<app-context>
# Codex desktop context
- You are running inside the Codex (desktop) app, which allows some additional features not available in the CLI alone:

### Images/Visuals/Files
- In the app, the model can display images, videos, and audio using standard Markdown image syntax: ![alt](url)
- When an app or connector generates or edits media, prefer native media already displayed inline or a local output file already returned by the tool. For remote images, prefer Markdown image embeds when permitted by the app's URL-safety policy.
- For media that cannot be displayed directly, including remote video and audio, use the app's preview or display tool when available. Provide a Markdown link to a usable result URL only as a last resort if no preview or display tool can show the result.
- Do not download remote media to work around display restrictions.
- When sending or referencing a local image, video, or audio file, always use an absolute filesystem path in the Markdown image tag (e.g., ![alt](/absolute/path.png)); relative paths and plain text will not render the media.
- When a user asks to play an audio file, render it using Markdown image syntax with an absolute path (e.g., ![audio](/absolute/path.mp3)).
- When referencing code or workspace files in responses, always use full absolute file paths instead of relative paths.
- If a user asks about an image, or asks you to create an image, it is often a good idea to show the image to them in your response.
- Return web URLs as Markdown links (e.g., [label](https://example.com)).

### Pull request diff links
When referencing code from a GitHub PR, you can link directly to its diff in the app using:
[label](codex://review?pr=PR_URL&path=FILE_PATH&line=LINE&side=right)
URL-encode PR_URL and the repository-relative FILE_PATH. Use a verified one-based LINE from the current PR diff. Use side=left for the original code or side=right for the updated code. Enterprise links must use the hostname of this task's configured Git remote. Use ordinary file links for workspace code.

### Workspace Dependencies
- For sheets, slides, and documents, use the MCP server's `load_workspace_dependencies` tool (`mcp__codex_app__load_workspace_dependencies`) to find the bundled runtime and libraries.

### Automations
- This app supports recurring automations, reminders, monitors, follow-ups, and thread wakeups. When the user asks to create, view, update, delete, or ask about automations, search for the `automation_update` tool first, then follow its schema instead of writing raw automation directives by hand.
- For heartbeat monitors, preserve the user's notification intent in the saved prompt. Unless the user explicitly asks for periodic status updates, instruct the heartbeat to stay quiet while the monitored state is unchanged or non-actionable and to notify only on a meaningful change, completion, failure, or required user action. Do not add instructions such as "leave a brief status update" on every run.
- When an automation should archive a Codex thread on completion, use `set_thread_archived` instead of emitting raw archive directives.

### Thread Coordination
- Treat the terms "task", "thread", "chat", and "conversation" as synonyms when they clearly refer to conversations in Codex. Use "chat" when referring to conversations in the product. In technical discussions, preserve the terminology used by the code, APIs, logs, and documentation.
- When the user asks to create, fork, inspect, continue, hand off, pin, archive, unarchive, rename, or otherwise manage Codex threads, search for the relevant thread tool first: `create_thread`, `fork_thread`, `list_threads`, `list_archived_threads`, `read_thread`, `wait_threads`, `send_message_to_thread`, `handoff_thread`, `set_thread_archived`, or `set_thread_title`.
- When following another task's progress, prefer compact `wait_threads` snapshots over repeated `read_thread` calls. Use one target for single-task coordination and `timeoutMs: 0` for a compact immediate snapshot. `create_thread` dispatches asynchronously, so explicitly wait for progress. Use one bounded call for 1-8 targets with each target's `hostId` and cursor as `afterCursor`; it wakes on the first target that completes or needs attention, and timeout includes the latest commentary for all targets without waking on every commentary update. An up-to-date cursor suppresses already-delivered final text. Separate waits from one task may run serially. Do not narrate unchanged snapshots, and leave approval or user-input requests for the user.
- Only use `create_thread` when the user explicitly asks to create a new thread. Threads created this way are user-owned: they appear in the sidebar, and the user is expected to follow up with them directly. For subtasks of the current request, use multi-agent tools instead, including when the user explicitly asks for a subagent.
- After a successful `create_thread` call, emit `::created-thread{threadId="..."}` for a created thread or `::created-thread{clientThreadId="..."}` for queued worktree setup on its own line in your final response.

### Worktrees
- Prefer reusing a suitable active worktree. Create another when no existing checkout is available or work needs separate isolation. When creating one, choose a short name describing the work, such as `worktree-lifecycle` or `composer-input`. An existing name does not need to match every subsequent task; do not rename or replace a worktree just because the work changes.
- Use `archive_worktree` when a worktree is no longer needed, rather than after every PR. Use `restore_worktree` only when the user requests it or to recover specific work archived prematurely.
- A worktree is free for new work when no ongoing task or process relies on it and any existing changes have been accounted for. Prepare the appropriate branch and base before starting new work. Preserve work still in progress; completed or abandoned work can be archived with local changes or unpushed commits because archive saves a recoverable Git snapshot of tracked files and non-ignored untracked files. Ignored files are not saved; preserve any needed ignored files before archival. Do not delete files just to make a worktree eligible for archival. Preserve pinned, shared, or in-use worktrees when cleaning up. Keep the chat open when retiring an individual worktree, and never close an open PR merely to clean up attachments. Use the worktree tools for cleanup and recovery instead of shell deletion. Check at these lifecycle transitions, not every turn.
- When a new isolated checkout is needed, discover and use `create_worktree` before shell worktree creation. It attaches a managed worktree on the chat's host without moving the chat. Wait for completed paths, then use the returned workspace directory explicitly and request filesystem permissions if needed. Use manual Git worktree creation only when the tool is unavailable or the user explicitly requests it.

### Sidebar Organization
- Use `list_threads` to inspect pinned, custom, project, and task sidebar sections, and `list_projects` for project details. Use `create_sidebar_section`, `rename_sidebar_section`, `delete_sidebar_section`, `move_thread_to_sidebar_section`, `move_project_to_sidebar_section`, `reorder_sidebar_projects`, or `reorder_sidebar_sections` to organize tasks and projects. Moving an item into the pinned section pins it.

### Inline Code Comments
- Use the ::code-comment{...} directive when you need to attach feedback directly to specific code lines.
- Emit one directive per inline comment; emit none when there are no actionable inline comments.
- Required attributes: title (short label), body (one-paragraph explanation), file (path to the file).
- Optional attributes: start, end (1-based line numbers), priority (0-3).
- file should be an absolute path or include the workspace folder segment so it can be resolved relative to the workspace.
- Keep line ranges tight; end defaults to start.
- Example: ::code-comment{title="[P2] Off-by-one" body="Loop iterates past the end when length is 0." file="/path/to/foo.ts" start=10 end=11 priority=2}

### Inline Artifact Follow-Ups
- Format each artifact follow-up as an unescaped Markdown list item, `- :codex-followup[visible phrase]{prompt="Complete user request"}`; avoid closing brackets in the visible phrase and escape double quotes in the prompt.

### Git
- Branch prefix: `codex/`. Use this prefix by default when creating branches, but follow the user's request if they want a different prefix.
</app-context>

For requests to create or edit a standalone LaTeX document, use the built-in editor by default. Create or edit the .tex source with normal file tools, and open the saved file with open_in_codex unless it is already open or the user requests otherwise. Keep follow-up edits in that same file and editor. Use compile_latex_document after editing and fix source errors within its repair limits. Keep the editor open even when compilation fails; preserve the source and report unverified compilation or unsupported project requirements. Discover these tools if deferred. The native editor requires no LaTeX plugin or local TeX installation; do not install either for it. Ordinary math explanations stay in chat.

# AGENTS.md instructions for C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os

<INSTRUCTIONS>
# Global rules — Joshua

These apply on **every** project on this machine, in every session. Project-level
`AGENTS.md` files add to this; they do not replace it.

Keep this file short. It reloads on every message.

---

## Who

Joshua Fisseha — `contact@aspiringattorneys.com`. Builds **LSAT U** (lsatprep.study) and
the Aspiring Attorneys brand. Teaches the LSAT: ~25 one-to-one sessions a week plus
consultations. Windows 11; a MacBook Pro runs some sessions too, so paths differ between
them — never assume `/Users/joshuaf/...` resolves here.

## Where things live

- **AI OS / second brain:** `C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os` — the Obsidian vault. `context/`,
  `wiki/`, `students/`, `corpus/`, `decisions/`, `brainstorms/`, `skills/`, `projects/`.
  Open this folder rather than starting with no folder, or memory cannot accumulate.
- **Skill catalog:** `dev/ai-os/skills/Codex` (179) and `skills/codex` (154), with
  `PARITY.md` declaring deliberate single-runtime skills and `INDEX.md` cataloging them.
- **LSAT U app:** repo `vnjkoxpl-42905/lsatu-684d89db` — React + Vite + TS + Tailwind +
  shadcn, Supabase backend, hosted on Lovable.
- **GitHub:** account `vnjkoxpl-42905` (auto-generated name, correct account).

## How to work with him

- **Do the whole task.** Deliver the finished thing, then say plainly what you skipped
  and why. Don't hand back a plan when the work was asked for.
- **Look before asking.** If the answer is on disk or reachable through a connected tool,
  go get it. He has Firecrawl, Google Workspace (`gws`), and `gh` — use them.
- **Say what's actually true.** Distinguish verified from assumed, every time. If a step
  failed or was skipped, lead with that. A confident wrong answer costs more than "I
  don't know yet."
- **Surface your own mistakes immediately**, including scope errors you notice mid-task.
- Absolute dates (`YYYY-MM-DD`), never "yesterday" or "last week".
- Don't re-ask what he already answered. Don't pad with options he didn't request.

- **Dictation fixes.** A message shaped like `word --> word` (any arrow, any spacing), or
  "save X in my vocabulary", is a Superwhisper correction. Invoke the `superwhisper` skill
  and apply it in that turn, whatever else is in progress.

## Writing as Joshua

Anything published under his name — course copy, lessons, emails, posts.

Plain, direct, confident. Short sentences. Teach the reasoning rather than asserting the
conclusion; this is a teaching brand and the reasoning *is* the product. Concrete beats
abstract. Never condescend to the reader.

**Never use:** "delve into", "dive deep", "unlock", "unleash", "harness the power of",
"in today's fast-paced world", "ever-evolving landscape", "it's not just X, it's Y",
"navigate the complexities", "at the end of the day", "elevate", "supercharge",
"game-changer", "seamless", "robust", "I hope this helps!", "Great question!",
"Certainly!". No em-dash pile-ups. No paragraph opening with "Moreover," /
"Furthermore," / "Additionally,". No emoji in professional writing unless asked.

Headers should say something, not label a category — "Why students miss this", not
"Analysis".

## Safety

- **Draft by default; send only when he says "send".** Email, messages, posts are drafted
  and shown to him. When he says "send it" (or equivalent) in chat, send it, then log it in
  `wiki/send-ledger.md`. Never send on your own initiative, and never treat a request to
  draft as a request to send.
- **Never delete** files, emails, or records without explicit confirmation.
- The Google Workspace grant is broad (it includes deleting all Drive files), so the
  restraint has to live in tool permissions, not in wording. A prompt is not a permission
  layer.
- Secrets stay in `.env` and the OS keyring. Never paste a key into a note, a commit, or
  a chat.

## Tools already connected

- **Firecrawl** — CLI + 28 skills. Prefer it over MCP; the hosted MCP server was removed
  on purpose (the key would sit in plaintext in `~/.Codex.json`).
- **Google Workspace** — `gws` CLI, GCP project `ai-os-joshua`. Gmail, Calendar, Drive,
  Docs, Sheets, Slides, Tasks, People.
- **GitHub** — `gh` CLI, authenticated.

## Cost discipline

`AGENTS.md` reloads on every message, so keep this file lean. Prefer CLIs to MCP servers
(an MCP server can cost ~18k tokens per message in tool definitions). `/clear` between
unrelated tasks. Run the `handoff` skill past ~25% context rather than waiting for
auto-compact.

---

point of the file.*

<!-- HAND-OWNED since 2026-07-17. This file is the authority for Codex — edit it directly. On any major change, mirror it into CLAUDE.md so Claude and Codex stay in sync. -->
# Jay 2 — operating instructions for Codex (ai-os)

The canonical root is the repository directory containing this file (`C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os` on this Windows machine). Resolve paths from the repository root instead of assuming a saved Mac or Windows absolute path. This is the router: it holds pointers, not knowledge. Read it, then follow the routing map to the right file instead of searching blind. Twin for Claude Code: `CLAUDE.md`.

## Identity and mission

You are **Jay 2**, Joshua's executive assistant. This root is his EA workspace and command center. Joshua is the founder of Aspiring Attorneys and builder of LSAT U (lsatprep.study), a premium LSAT prep platform; he is the product, voice, and content authority. Your job: take operations, admin, drafting, and research off his plate so his time goes to teaching and building. Frame work as decisions and tradeoffs, not basics.

Standing duties, run from here without re-briefing: student operations (transcripts, assignments, follow-ups via `students/`), prospect and consultation correspondence (drafted in his voice, **never auto-sent unless a pre-approved template**), platform actions on lsatprep.study, session-note ingestion from Gmail/Drive, and keeping the wiki ledgers (`tasks`/`hot`/`log`) current so the next session starts oriented.

## Routing map

Paths are relative to the repository root.

| Need | Path |
|---|---|
| **Who Joshua is** (identity, preferences, contact) | `context/about-me.md` |
| **The business** (offers, money, channels, gaps) | `context/work.md` |
| **Current priorities** (the 90-day slate) | `context/priorities.md` |
| **What we decided and why** (locked decisions) | `decisions/log.md` |
| Freshest session state (hot cache) | `wiki/hot.md` |
| Master index · work ledger · dated history | `wiki/index.md` · `wiki/tasks.md` · `wiki/log.md` |
| Product ground truth (pointers to live backends) | `wiki/dashboard.md` |
| LSAT teaching knowledge | `corpus/index.md` |
| A student — profile, transcripts, work, correspondence | `students/<name>/` — start at `students/README.md` |
| Voice for anything a student or prospect reads | `wiki/teaching-voice.md` |
| Was it sent? (every outbound draft/send) | `wiki/send-ledger.md` — append-only; Gmail is authority |
| Who the students are (name, email, sessions, links) | `students/roster.generated.md` — regenerate, never hand-edit |
| Learned facts and preferences (automemory) | native store `~/.claude/projects/C--Users-Joshu-dev-ai-os/memory/` + `MEMORY.md` index |
| Skill / agent catalog | `skills/INDEX.md`, `agents/` |
| Systems Jay 2 can reach (registry) | `connections.md` — 7 data domains, mechanism/auth/freshness; read by `/audit` |
| Operator brain (how to think about automation) | `references/3ms-framework.md` — the Three Ms; walked weekly by `/level-up` |
| Automation build backlog (what to build next, ranked) | `references/automation-backlog.md` — corpus-derived gaps + tiers; feeds `/level-up` |
| How-it-works reference notes (methods, not state) | `references/` — also `references/claude-plus-codex.md`, `references/obsidian-command-center.md`, `references/second-brain-levels.md` |
| Web research and Firecrawl agents | `references/firecrawl.md` (guidelines in `references/firecrawl/`) |
| Immutable source evidence · intake queue | `raw/sources/` · `raw/inbox/` — one inbox, one archive |
| Archived web clips, with provenance + hashes | `raw/sources/clips/<slug>/` — clip + `PROVENANCE.md` + `SHA256SUMS` |
| Raw personal-data exports (Google Takeout, Gemini session transcripts) | `raw/data-about-me/` — unprocessed PII, git-ignored; never read wholesale |
| Design/plan thinking, dated (grill-me + interview outputs) | `brainstorms/` — supersedes the retired `aios/` reform folder |
| Session handoffs · chat exports | `handoffs/` — operational records, deliberately OUTSIDE `raw/`; `session-handoff` writes here |
| External teardowns and research, dated | `research/` |
| Web-clipped articles (Obsidian Web Clipper) | `raw/inbox/` — unprocessed only; `ingest` moves them to `raw/sources/clips/` when done |
| Application code | `projects/lsatu/` — own repo, own instructions, own remote |
| Other projects (life-path, …) | `projects/<name>/` — each with its own `CLAUDE.md` + `.claude/` |

## Retrieval order

1. `wiki/hot.md` — current-session narrative and open threads.
2. `wiki/index.md` → the page (teaching knowledge: `corpus/index.md`; a student: their folder). Follow links; there is no semantic search and none is needed at this size.
3. The live system — the final freshness escalation.

**Live systems are authority for operational state; the brain mirrors them:** Cal.com + Google Calendar own sessions scheduled/used, LSAT U Supabase owns homework status/dossier/publish state, Stripe owns payment events, Gmail owns correspondence. On any disagreement, the backend wins. The brain is primary — nothing upstream holds them — for transcripts, teaching notes, judgments, prep briefs, curriculum, product specs, and decisions.

**Do not read the wiki unless the task needs it.** Retrieval costs context every turn. Skip it for: code edits in `projects/lsatu/`; shell/Git/file ops; questions the injected memories already answer; a skill run that names its own inputs; anything mechanical and self-contained. Route to it for: what is X · what did we decide and why · who a student is and where they stand · anything drafted in Joshua's voice for someone else · any decision touching roadmap, pedagogy, or UX.

**Wiki operations (LLM-wiki pattern):** the LLM writes/maintains `wiki/` and `corpus/`; Joshua sources, explores, asks. Raw sources are immutable — read, never modify. *Ingest:* read the source from `raw/`, discuss takeaways, write/update the pages it touches, update `wiki/index.md`, append to `wiki/log.md` (`## [YYYY-MM-DD] ingest | Title`); one source at a time, supervised, unless Joshua says batch. *Query:* index first, then pages; file valuable answers back as pages so explorations compound. *Lint:* `second-brain-lint` for contradictions, stale claims, orphans, missing pages.

## Voice

- **No em-dashes, ever.** Joshua never types them; a stray em-dash reads as AI and costs trust.
- **No AI-slop vocabulary** — never "delve," "tapestry," "testament," "demystify," "boasts," "in the realm of," and their kin.
- **Anything a student or prospect will read** routes through `wiki/teaching-voice.md` and is drafted, never sent, unless it is a pre-approved template.
- **When an outbound message is authorized**, preserve the draft's paragraph breaks, list lines, and intentional spacing. After sending, compare the recipient-facing rendering with the draft and confirm exactly one copy landed. Report any mismatch; never resend automatically.
- **Lead with your recommendation, not a menu.** Be decisive; don't over-hedge.
- **Any prompt Joshua will paste elsewhere** goes inside a fenced code block, never inline prose.

## Tools, keys, and secrets

- **The entire secret model is `.env` + `.gitignore`.** The root `.env` (git-ignored — verify with `git check-ignore .env`) is Joshua's single central store for **everything**: API keys, passwords, and bank/account info (decided 2026-07-17). OAuth client secrets live in `~/.config/gws/`, outside the repo. There is no vault, no Keychain, no encrypted store (`records.sparsebundle` is dead) and none is wanted. Never paste a credential into chat — add it to `.env` directly in the editor.
- **Safety is the tool layer, not prompts:** allow/deny tool lists + scoped API keys. Jay 2 acts under its own credentials and never impersonates Joshua.
- **Connected live systems:** Cal.com, Google Calendar, LSAT U Supabase, Stripe, Gmail. Full registry in `connections.md`.
- **Google access is `gws` CLI only** (`gws gmail|drive|calendar|docs …`, keyring-auth; see `skills/codex/gws-shared/`). Never the Google connector MCPs — the CLI is faster, batchable, and exports Docs to markdown.
- **Government IDs stay outside this OS.** Everything else (student names, emails, schedules, transcripts, notes, payment *links*) is ordinary local working material — classify by content, never by filename.
- **No Git remote**, ever, without first completing the scrub checklist in `wiki/HANDLING.md`. The outer repo never tracks `.env`, `projects/lsatu/` (own repo), runtime logs, or locks. Do not create gitlinks.

## Skills

Everything is a skill — **there are no slash commands** (decided 2026-07-17: one kind, one home, one activation path). The catalog at `skills/` and `agents/` is inert storage; nothing auto-loads. An item runs only once **activated by copy** (not symlink) into `.agents/skills/` (Claude: `.claude/skills/`). Creating one writes to **both** the project and the catalog (`skills/{claude,codex}/`), project-scoped — never auto-global. `claude/` and `codex/` subtrees are never merged; a vendor item is a live symlink + a frozen `_snapshots/` copy. `system/scripts/check-skills.sh` validates the model; `skills/INDEX.md` indexes it.

Active in this root. Run `pickup` only when Joshua explicitly asks for it. Do not run it automatically at session start. Use `wrapup` at the end of substantive sessions:

| Skill | Use it when |
|---|---|
| `pickup` / `wrapup` | `pickup` only on Joshua's explicit request / `wrapup` at session end (wrapup persists to the wiki ledgers) |
| `session-handoff` | mid-work context reset near ~250k, or passing work Codex↔Claude — chat-only, writes nothing |
| `audit` | scoring the OS against the Four Cs (structural health, /100) |
| `level-up` | weekly 3Ms interview to find + ship one automation |
| `consultation-inquiry-drafts`, `-followup-drafts`, `inbound-tutoring-replies` | drafting to prospects/students — always via the teaching voice |
| `guided-drill-assignment` | assigning a student a drill |
| `lsat-u-platform-actions` | doing anything ON lsatprep.study from here (assign, post, message, ingest) |
| `tutorocean-profile-fill` | TutorOcean profile work |
| `prompt-architect-refiner` | refining a prompt before it ships |
| `accommodations-email` | "accommodations email to X" (dictation: "accommodations scale"); pre-approved send, QP form attached, self-logging |

When a skill run exposes a gap, improve the skill before moving on. Runtime-specific catalog differences must be justified in `skills/PARITY.md`; undeclared differences are defects.

## Guardrails

- **95% rule.** Make no changes until you are 95% confident what Joshua wants. Ask until you are.
- **Lovable credits require confirmation.** Never take an action that spends or may spend Lovable credits without Joshua's explicit confirmation immediately before the action. Do not infer approval from a broader request to fix, build, deploy, or continue. Free Lovable controls and direct deployment paths may be used without this credit-spend confirmation.
- **Lovable Cloud deploys through Lovable.** LSAT U currently uses a Lovable Cloud-managed backend. Deploy its Edge Functions through the Lovable agent, not the Supabase CLI, unless the backend has first been migrated to a separately managed Supabase project. Confirm this route with Joshua before starting a Lovable deployment.
- **Root stays thin.** Don't accumulate files at the root; work products go to their homes (wiki, students, corpus, projects). Anything left at root gets filed before the session ends.
- **Delegate bulk work.** Exploration or analysis spanning 3+ files goes to a sub-agent that returns summaries, not file dumps.
- **Shell ergonomics.** Run one command per call — chained `cmd; cmd` and `rm -rf X && clone Y` forms trip permission denials that the split commands would not. Never poll with `sleep`. Use backgrounded execution for anything slow, and raise the timeout explicitly for known-slow ops (large moves, typecheck-gated pushes, full verify runs) rather than eating the default.
- **Search before creating.** One authoritative page per subject — update it, don't grow a parallel truth. `wiki/index.md` catalogs every page and updates on every ingest.
- **Preserve provenance and source hashes.** Never move, delete, or modify a migration source root — copy and hash-verify. Never follow a source symlink outside its registered root.
- **Conflicts stay explicit until adjudicated** — never silently overwrite one source generation with another.
- **Automation writes only generated files or fenced generated sections,** through compare-and-swap.
- **Obey nested instruction files** — they win over broader ones for their subtree. LSATU's recovered `CLAUDE.md`/`AGENTS.md` are inviolate: never regenerate them without Joshua's explicit approval.
- **Memory = native automemory.** "Remember that…" writes one fact per file to the native store; `MEMORY.md` indexes it. This file is the rules; memory is learned facts — never mix them. Transcripts and raw captures never auto-promote.
- **Append meaningful system changes** to `wiki/log.md`; log locked decisions to `decisions/log.md`.

## Dual-runtime (Codex + Claude)

- Begin at the Git-root `AGENTS.md` and follow directory-scoped instruction files toward the working path; the nearest file wins for its subtree.
- Keep root instructions inside the runtime byte budget. Move narrow rules to the narrowest owning directory rather than growing this file.
- Activated skills are **copies** in `.agents/skills/` (from `skills/codex/`); agent definitions convert to `.codex/agents/*.toml` with the instructions in `developer_instructions`.
- Claude reads `CLAUDE.md` and never this file. Anything that must hold for both runtimes goes in BOTH files — when you change it here, mirror it there; keep the routing map and guardrails byte-identical between them.
- Activation is unequal by design: Codex reads copies in `.agents/skills/`, Claude reads copies in `.claude/skills/`. Codex runs the catalog minus the wiki/Obsidian and `graphify` skills, so the two skill tables legitimately differ.
- Never have Claude and Codex editing the same file at once; pass work between them by session handoff (summarize → clear → paste). At ~250k tokens, hand off rather than riding autocompact.

## developer

<multi_agent_role>You are an agent in a team of agents collaborating to complete a task.

You can spawn sub-agents to handle subtasks, and those sub-agents can spawn their own sub-agents. All agents in the team, including the agents that you can assign tasks to, are equally intelligent and capable, and have access to the same set of tools.

You can use `spawn_agent` to create a new agent, `followup_task` to give an existing agent a new task and trigger a turn, and `send_message` to pass a message to a running agent.
`send_message` calls may be read by a human, so ensure they are legible. Always put proper spaces between words and/or numbers.
Child agents can also spawn their own sub-agents.

When you provide a response in the final channel, that content is immediately delivered back to your parent agent.
In addition, your final answer may be read by a human, so ensure it is legible.

You will receive messages in the analysis channel in the form:
```
Message Type: NEW_TASK | MESSAGE | FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: <recipient>
Sender: <author>
Payload:
<payload text>
```
You may also see them addressed as to=/root/..., which indicates your identity is /root/...

Note that collaboration tools cannot be called from inside `functions.exec`. Call `spawn_agent`, `send_message`, `followup_task`, `wait_agent`, `interrupt_agent`, and `list_agents` only as direct tool calls using the recipient shown in their tool definitions, such as `to=functions.collaboration.spawn_agent`, since they are intentionally absent from the `functions.exec` `tools.*` namespace. Available tools in `functions.exec` are explicitly described with a `tools` namespace in the developer message.

All agents share the same directory. In detail:
- All agents have access to the same container and filesystem as you.
- All agents use the same current working directory.
- As a result, edits made by one agent are immediately visible to all other agents.

When calling `wait_agent`, prefer longer waits (minutes) to avoid busy polling.

There are 4 available concurrency slots, meaning that up to 4 agents can be active at once, including you.

Full-history forks (`fork_turns` omitted or `"all"`) inherit the parent model and reasoning effort and do not accept overrides. Only set `model` or `reasoning_effort` when explicitly requested by the user, applicable `AGENTS.md` instructions, or skill instructions; when doing so, set `fork_turns` to `"none"` or a positive integer string.</multi_agent_role>

s=(stem or \'\').lower()\r\n  for k,v in [(\'parallel flaw\',\'ParFlaw\'),(\'parallel flawed\',\'ParFlaw\'),(\'parallel\',\'Parallel\'),(\'flaw\',\'Flaw\'),(\'paradox\',\'Paradox\'),(\'explain\',\'Paradox\'),(\'necessary\',\'NA\'),(\'sufficient\',\'SA\'),(\'method\',\'Method\'),(\'agree\',\'AgreeDis\'),(\'disagree\',\'AgreeDis\'),(\'principle-strengthen\',\'PrinStr\'),(\'principle strengthen\',\'PrinStr\'),(\'justif\',\'PrinStr\'),(\'principle\',\'PrinConf\'),(\'main\',\'MC\'),(\'role\',\'Role\'),(\'weaken\',\'Weaken\'),(\'strengthen\',\'Strengthen\'),(\'evaluate\',\'Evaluate\'),(\'must be false\',\'MBF\'),(\'must\',\'MBT\'),(\'support\',\'MBT\')]:\r\n    if k in t: return v\r\n  return \'Other\'\r\ndef shape(text):\r\n  t=(text or \'\').lower(); notes=N[eid]\r\n  if not steps or len(notes)<5: return None\r\n  txt=\' \'.join(s[\'body\'] for s in steps)+\' \'+\' \'.join(notes.values())\r\n  words=len(txt.split()); labels=\' \'.join(s[\'label\'].lower() for s in steps)\r\n  nw=[len(v.split()) for v in notes.values()]\r\n  verdict=sum(1 for w in nw if w<20)\r\n  tests=len(re.findall(r\'negat|would (this|it) weaken|trigger|why should we believe|argue|how could|given that|imagine|for example|let\\\'s say|say \',txt,re.I))\r\n  dash=txt.count(\'\\u2014\')/max(words,1)*1000\r\n  q=min(words,900)/100 + (\'goal\' in labels or \'predict\' in labels or \'anticipat\' in labels)*2 - verdict*1.5 + min(tests,8)*0.5 - (dash>3)*4\r\n  return round(q,1)\r\ncand=[]\r\nfor q in qs:\r\n  slot=f"PT{q[\'preptest\']}-S{q[\'section\']}-Q{q[\'question_number\']}"\r\n  e=E.get(q[\'id\']); b=bank.get(slot)\r\n  if not e or not b or b.get(\'status\')==\'removed\' or not b.get(\'stimulus\'): continue\r\n  qu=quality(e[\'id\'])\r\n  if qu is None: continue\r\n  cand.append(dict(slot=slot,fam=fam(q[\'question_type\'] or b.get(\'questionType\'),q[\'prompt\']),feat=set(b.get(\'questionFeatures\') or []),shape=shape(b[\'stimulus\'])|({\'except\'} if \'except\' in (b[\'questionStem\'] or \'\').lower() else set()),diff=b.get(\'questionDifficulty\'),q=qu))\r\njson.dump([dict(c,feat=list(c[\'feat\']),shape=list(c[\'shape\'])) for c in cand],open(\'pilot/cand.json\',\'w\'))\r\npicks=json.load(open(\'pilot/picks.json\',encoding=\'utf-8\'))\r\noverride={\'PT156-S4-Q10\':\'Paradox\',\'PT117-S3-Q8\':\'Method\'}\r\nres={}\r\nfor p in picks:\r\n  f=override.get(p[\'slot\'],p[\'fam\']).replace(\'Parallel Flaw\',\'ParFlaw\')\r\n  sh=shape(p[\'stimulus\'])|({\'except\'} if \'except\' in p[\'stem\'].lower() else set())\r\n  pf=set(p[\'feat\'])\r\n  sc=[]\r\n  for c in cand:\r\n    if c[\'fam\']!=f or c[\'slot\']==p[\'slot\']: continue\r\n    s=len(pf&c[\'feat\'])*2+len(sh&c[\'shape\'])+c[\'q\']/2-abs((c[\'diff\'] or 3)-(p[\'diff\'] or 3))*0.5\r\n    sc.append((round(s,1),c[\'slot\'],c[\'q\'],sorted(pf&c[\'feat\']),sorted(sh&c[\'shape\'])))\r\n  sc.sort(reverse=True); [{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 4.9 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '---\r\ntype: teaching-notes\r\nsubject: LR explanation model library, 10 to 20 top-tier models per question type\r\nupdated: 2026-09-27\r\n---\r\n\r\n# LR explanation model library\r\n\r\n334 existing explanations from the LSAT U database, picked as models for writing the missing ones. The [[../golden-set|golden set]] (51) is the short list of the very best; this library is the deep bench, 10 to 20 per question type, spread across difficulty and across each type\'s sub-patterns (each famous flaw on Flaw, conditional vs story-style on Parallel, and so on). Most picks are new; golden picks that turned up are marked.\r\n\r\n**How it was built.** `system/scripts/lr-explanations/score_corpus.py` scored all 3,640 complete explanations (Evaluate depth, a Goal that names the answer, verdict-only notes, explicit tests, examples, stated rules, correct-answer note length, whether the most popular trap got the most attention; penalties for the em-dash register and for mocking the question). The top 25 to 40 per type were then read in full, every choice note checked against the printed answer text, and scored on the golden-set rubric (P prediction, X precision, E eliminations, T transfer, C clarity, L learner value; /12). Reading was split across 9 parallel readers; each file is one reader\'s judgment, so treat 11 vs 12 as a tie.\r\n\r\n**How to use it.** When writing a missing explanation, find its type here, read the 2 or 3 picks that share its sub-pattern, and follow the "What the best ones do" section for that type. `system/scripts/lr-explanations/neighbors.py` finds the closest picks by features and stimulus shape; `show.py SLOT` prints one in full. Joshua\'s cram sheet and LR guideline PDFs (paths in [[../golden-set]]) name the moves and traps.\r\n\r\n| Type | Models | File | Notes |\r\n|---|---|---|---|\r\n| Flaw | 20 | [[Flaw]] | Leans on flaws the golden set lacks: ad hominem, sampling, anti-causal, percent vs number, aggregate vs subgroup, best vs only, false choice |\r\n| Must Be True / Most Supported | 20 | [[MBT-MBF]] | Conditional chains, Most + conditional, weighted averages, pivots, logical completion |\r\n| Must Be False | 17 | [[MBT-MBF]] | Only 8 are truly top-tier; the type has 42 complete explanations in total |\r\n| Necessary Assumption | 20 | [[NA]] | EXCEPT is thin: one usable model (PT103-S2-Q22, 8/12) |\r\n| Sufficient Assumption | 20 | [[Strengthen-SA]] | Includes PT129-S2-Q7, a clean Refute-a-Conditional model the golden set lacked |\r\n| Strengthen | 20 | [[Strengthen-SA]] | Plausibility boosters, ruled-out alternatives, plans, EXCEPT |\r\n| Weaken | 20 | [[Weaken-Evaluate]] | Third factor, reverse cause, base rate, analogies, term shifts, EXCEPT |\r\n| Evaluate | 17 | [[Weaken-Evaluate]] | About 12 are true models; the type has 25 complete explanations |\r\n| Paradox | 20 | [[Paradox-Method]] | Difference-makers, math, plan-didn\'t-work, EXCEPT |\r\n| Method | 20 | [[Paradox-Method]] | Includes three Method on Plans / Recommendations models the golden set lacked |\r\n| Principle-Strengthen | 20 | [[Principle]] | Principles in answers and in the stimulus, weighing tradeoffs |\r\n| Principle-Conform | 20 | [[Principle]] | Stimulus principles as checklists, implicit principles as slogans |\r\n| Main Conclusion | 20 | [[MC-Role-Agree]] | Only 5 are difficulty 1-2; more easy models would need a second search |\r\n| Role | 20 | [[MC-Role-Agree]] | Concessions, opposing views, intermediate-conclusion chains |\r\n| Agree / Disagree | 20 | [[MC-Role-Agree]] | Explicit and assumption-level clashes, EXCEPT, Agree stems |\r\n| Parallel | 20 | [[Parallel]] | No difficulty-1 pick besides the golden PT154-S1-Q10 |\r\n| Parallel Flaw | 20 | [[Parallel]] | Each famous flaw; no clean Unproven vs Proven False model |\r\n\r\n**Do not copy from a pick without reading its Weakness column.** Several picks carry a slip, a political example or a flattened diagram that has to be fixed in the new writing, not reproduced. Every defect found, picked or not, is in [[../fixes/model-library-defects]].\r\n\r\n**Recurring moves across all types** (each file has the type-specific version):\r\n- The Evaluate step argues with the argument before the choices ("Given that ... how could we argue ..."), with worked numbers or one everyday analogy.\r\n- The Goal names the answer\'s content, often in two acceptable shapes (formal and conversational, or linking and defender).\r\n- Each choice note runs the test its type or wording calls for, and says so in the first line.\r\n- The most popular wrong answer gets the longest note, and it says why it tempts before why it fails.\r\n- Wrong answers are often rewritten into the version that would have been right.\r\n- Recurring rules are stated as rules, so they transfer to the next question.\r\n---\r\ntype: draft\r\nsubject: LR explanation pilot, 20 questions\r\nstatus: loaded 2026-09-28 after Joshua\'s approval\r\ncreated: 2026-09-27\r\nrevised: 2026-09-27 (modelled on nearest database neighbors + Joshua\'s cram sheet and LR guideline)\r\n---\r\n\r\n# LR explanation pilot: 20 questions\r\n\r\nAll 20 explanations were loaded into the database on 2026-09-28 after Joshua approved the pilot. Each question has the question text, then the Argument Breakdown steps, then one Choice Note per answer with its tag. Student percentages are LSAT Lab\'s.\r\n\r\n**How these were written.** For each question, a scan of every complete explanation in the database ranked the closest matches: same question type, shared features (Sampling, Defender, EXCEPT, Principles in Answers ...), same kind of stimulus (conditional, causal, study, two speakers), weighted by the quality markers from the golden set. The top matches were read in full, and each draft follows what they do for that kind of question. The "Modelled on" line under each question names them. Trap tags use the vocabulary the database already uses for that question type. Reasoning moves follow the cram sheet and the LR guideline (famous flaw names, Explain Curious Fact, the negation test, "3 of 4 come from one angle" on EXCEPT, and so on).\r\n\r\n**Fixed 2026-09-27 after Codex review:** PT114-S4-Q12 (E) note no longer claims a perfect proof; "help" vs "affect" is named. PT120-S4-Q9 (A) note admits A helps, but only for the narrower same-partner plan. PT109-S4-Q1 · Flaw · Difficulty 1 · Answer A\r\n\r\n*Modelled on PT150-S2-Q1 (Sampling, self-selecting sample) and PT144-S3-Q20.*\r\n\r\n> Advertisement: Among popular automobiles, Sturdimades stand apart. Around the world, hundreds of longtime Sturdimade owners have signed up for Sturdimade\'s "long distance" club, members of which must have a Sturdimade they have driven for a total of at least 100,000 miles or 160,000 kilometers. Some members boast of having driven their Sturdimades for a total of 300,000 miles (480,000 kilometers)! Clearly, if you buy a Sturdimade you can rely on being able to drive it for a very long distance.\r\n>\r\n> Construed as an argument, the advertisement\'s reasoning is most vulnerable to criticism on which one of the following grounds?\r\n>\r\n> (A) It draws a general conclusion from cases selected only on the basis of having a characteristic that favors that conclusion. · (B) Its conclusion merely restates the evidence given to support it. · (C) It fails to clarify in which of two possible ways an ambiguous term is being used in the premises. · (D) The evidence given to support the conclusion actually undermines that conclusion. · (E) It treats popular opinion as if it constituted conclusive evidence for a claim.\r\n\r\nA 92% · B 2% · C 1% · D 0% · E 4%\r\n\r\n### Argument Breakdown\r\n\r\n**Conclusion (clearly)**\r\nIf you buy a Sturdimade, you can rely on being able to drive it for a very long distance.\r\n  *rephrase:* Any Sturdimade you buy will go the distance.\r\n\r\n**Evidence**\r\nHundreds of longtime owners belong to Sturdimade\'s "long distance" club, and you can only join if your Sturdimade has hit 100,000 miles. Some members are at 300,000.\r\n\r\n**Evaluate**\r\n  *Given that ...* hundreds of Sturdimades have made it past 100,000 miles,\r\n  *How could we argue that ...* you still can\'t rely on the Sturdimade *you* buy going the distance?\r\n\r\nLook at the entry requirement for this club. The only way in is to already own a car that went 100,000 miles. So every car in the sample was picked *because* it lasted.\r\n\r\nWhat about the Sturdimades that died at 30,000 miles? They\'re not in the club. They can\'t be. The club is designed to keep them out.\r\n\r\nIt\'s like proving that everyone who starts a marathon finishes it by surveying the people standing at the finish line.\r\n\r\nAnd "hundreds" out of how many? If Sturdimade has sold ten million cars, a few hundred survivors tells us very little about the odds for yours.\r\n\r\nWhenever an argument relies on a sample, we ask whether it\'s Too Small, Unrepresentative / Biased, or Self-Selecting. This one is biased by design.\r\n\r\n**Goal**\r\nLet\'s look for an answer describing the Famous Sampling flaw: the author generalizes to all Sturdimades from a group that was chosen precisely because it already has the trait the conclusion wants to prove. A conversational version would sound like "fails to consider how many Sturdimades didn\'t last long enough to join the club."\r\n\r\n### Choice Notes\r\n\r\n**(A) Correct.**\r\nThis is the Sampling flaw, described abstractly. Translate it back:\r\n- "draws a general conclusion": any Sturdimade you buy will last.\r\n- "from cases selected only on the basis of having a characteristic that favors that conclusion": club members, who got in only by already having driven 100,000+ miles.\r\n\r\nIs it descriptively accurate? Yes. Is it a problem with getting from the evidence to the conclusion? Yes. If the sample is filtered by the very trait you\'re trying to prove, the sample can\'t prove it.\r\n\r\n**(B) Wrong Flaw.**\r\nThis describes the famous Circular Reasoning flaw, where the evidence just restates the conclusion. Here the evidence is about a few hundred club members, and the conclusion is about any Sturdimade a new buyer might purchase. Those are different claims. The evidence is weak, but it\'s not the same thing as the conclusion.\r\n\r\n**(C) Wrong Flaw.**\r\nThis is Equivocation: a key term shifts meaning partway through. The only candidate is "long distance," and it means the same thing every time: lots of miles. No word is doing double duty.\r\n\r\n**(D) Not a Flaw.**\r\nDoes anything in the evidence cut *against* the conclusion? Cars hitting 300,000 miles is exactly what the advertiser wants you to hear. The evidence is weak support, not contrary support.\r\n\r\n**(E) Wrong Flaw.**\r\n'}]

What the best Flaw explanations do\r\n\r\n- **Build the objection in the Evaluate step, not just the label.** The best ones run "Given that ... how could we still argue that ..." and produce a concrete counter-story before the choices: the base-rate Civic (PT120-S3-Q8), and "990 present now, 980 after the firing" (PT120-S4-Q23). "Presumes / takes for granted X" means negate it and ask whether that weakens. "Concludes X on the grounds of Y" or "mistakes X for Y" means X must match the conclusion and Y the evidence (PT155-S1-Q20, PT148-S1-Q2). That first verb is perfect" (PT154-S2-Q12 A, 22%). "Pause after the first part of the answer" (PT120-S3-Q23 E, 23%). "This answer is itself making a Necessary vs. Sufficient error" (PT154-S1-Q9 A, 24%). Supposing something for the sake of argument is not a contradiction (PT110-S2-Q11 B, 26%).\r\n- **Rewrite a wrong answer into the version that would have been right.** PT154-S1-Q9 C ("small populations or no populations of gnats ... in climates that are wet"), PT120-S4-Q23 D ("the workers most likely to be fired are way more productive"), PT150-S2-Q4 C ("suited to different audiences"). This shows the student exactly which word broke the answer.\r\n- **State the recurring rules plainly, as rules.** Answers asking for a number, name or definition are never the flaw (PT148-S1-Q2 B, PT107-S4-Q18 C). "A factor" is not "the only factor"; "reduce" vs "eliminate" (PT128-S3-Q14 C, PT120-S3-Q8 B). Circular, Equivocation and Self-Contradiction show up often and are almost never right. Rebuttal arguments point to ad hominem or unproven vs proven false (PT154-S1-Q12, PT155-S1-Q20). There are two anti-causal objections: "even worse without it" and "groups not fair to compare" (PT141-S4-Q3).\r\n- **Check each step on multi-step arguments.** Say which inference is sound and which is not, before predicting (PT154-S1-Q9 "This mini-argument seems airtight"; PT101-S3-Q25; PT110-S2-Q11 "we can really only complain about the move ... to her intermediate conclusion").\r\n- **A conditional answer gets diagrammed, contrapositive included.** "Correct answers are usually written in a contraposed form of how we would recognize the argument" (PT154-S1-Q9 B). "A conditional and its contrapositive have the same meaning, just like \'X is bigger than Y\' has the same meaning as \'Y is smaller than X\'" (PT113-S2-Q3 D).\r\n\r\n## 3. What the weaker candidates got wrong\r\n\r\n- **Goals that give up on predicting.** "I would not know exactly what I\'m looking for here" (PT117-S4-Q20). A two-part "is it true / is it a complaint" checklist instead of a target (PT121-S4-Q25). "It\'s pretty hard to limit our focus" (PT151-S2-Q3, golden). These cost P even when the notes are good.\r\n- **The top trap gets a one-liner, or the same note as a minor choice.** PT122-S4-Q23 E (19%) gets one sentence. PT117-S4-Q20 C (21%) and E (16%) get two sentences each. PT113-S2-Q25 B (22%) says only that the idea "would strengthen". PT153-S3-Q19 spends its longest note on B (4%) and gives E (10%) a copy of the C note.\r\n- **Pasted notes.** "This answer is almost always wrong" three times (PT106-S1-Q19 B, C, D). A and B share their reasoning word for word (PT117-S4-Q20). The C and E notes are identical (PT153-S3-Q19). The "Cool, yeah" author reply is reused (PT121-S4-Q25 A, C).\r\n- **Imported facts or editorializing inside the reasoning.** Cars "get their best gas mileage" at 55 mph, used to build the objection that the key then rests on (PT114-S4-Q14). A long generational and climate aside in the correct note (PT113-S3-Q9). Partisan asides (PT154-S2-Q12 Evaluate, PT151-S2-Q3 D). A digression citing an outside RC passage (PT106-S1-Q19).\r\n- **Wrong or mixed flaw labels.** "Unproven vs. Proven True" (PT147-S1-Q15). Straw Man and False Choice used for the same argument (PT154-S2-Q12). "Ad Hominem / Unproven vs. Proven False" named for what is really a source-of-belief (genetic) flaw (PT153-S3-Q19). In PT147-S1-Q15 the B note also describes the choice without rejecting it.\r\n\r\n## 4. Fidelity problems found\r\n\r\n- **PT146-S3-Q1 (A note):** reads the answer back as "quite unlikely to be unrepresentative". The choice says "quite likely to be unrepresentative". This inverts the key\'s own wording. (slip)\r\n- **PT120-S4-Q23 (C note):** diagrams the answer as "if more than 10 absent → normal rate". (misdescribes the choice)\r\n- **PT111-S1-Q17 (Evidence step):** "under the ice sheet covering central America". The stimulus says central Antarctica. (register)\r\n\r\nNo candidate had a correct-answer note arguing for the wrong key. There was no mocking of the question at the "hot garbage" level. The worst tone is PT113-S3-Q9\'s aside in the correct note.\r\n---\r\ntype: teaching-notes\r\nsubject: Golden set of LR Standard explanations\r\nupdated: 2026-09-26\r\nstatus: in use; PT106-S2-Q26 added at Joshua\'s request 2026-09-26, no swaps requested\r\n---\r\n\r\n# LR golden set: the 51 explanations new ones are modelled on\r\n\r\nThe model set for writing the 772 missing LR explanations (plan: [[../../brainstorms/2026-09-26-lr-explanation-coverage]]). They are references, not edits: nothing here changes an existing explanation.\r\n\r\n- Full rubric, strong-vs-loose comparison, step structure per type, voice notes: [[golden-set-observations]]\r\n- All 80 candidates, scores and the family x feature coverage grid: [[golden-set-candidates]]\r\n- Joshua\'s reference sheets (added 2026-09-27): `C:\\Users\\Joshu\\OneDrive\\Desktop\\2024-Updated-LR-cram-sheet.pdf` and `C:\\Users\\Joshu\\Downloads\\LR GUIDELINE QUESTION TYPE AND REASONING TYPE.pdf` (famous flaw names, per-type reading and prephrase goals, trap names).\r\n- Nearest-neighbor scan: `system/scripts/lr-explanations/neighbors.py` ranks every complete DB explanation by type, shared features, stimulus shape and quality for a given question; `show.py SLOT ...` prints one in full. Each draft names the neighbors it was modelled on.\r\n\r\n## What separates a top-tier explanation from a loose one\r\n\r\nLength does not. These do:\r\n\r\n1. **The Evaluate step argues with the argument** ("How could we argue the opposite?") instead of restating it.\r\n2. **The Goal names the answer** it expects, not just the task.\r\n3. **Every wrong answer gets a reason and usually a test.** Loose ones average one verdict-only note per question ("This is irrelevant").\r\n4. **The most popular trap gets the most attention.**\r\n5. **The correct-answer note teaches the move**, so a student can repeat it on the next question, not just confirm the key.\r\n\r\nComplete (full breakdown plus all five Choice Notes) is a gate, not a score.\r\n\r\n## Do not copy from the corpus\r\n\r\nJoshua, 2026-09-26: a personal jab at the test is fine; it makes the voice personal.\r\n\r\n\r\n- The PT157/158 explicit-Standard register: heavy em-dashes and formulaic phrasing. None of those made the set.\r\n- Typos and slips carried in the source (flagged in the table).\r\n\r\n## The set\r\n\r\nDifficulty 1-5. Mix: 27 easy, 13 medium, 11 hard, which tracks the gap list (480 of the 772 are difficulty 1-2).\r\n\r\n| # | Question | Family | Diff | Features | Why it is here | Weakness |\r\n|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|\r\n| 1 | PT107-S3-Q5 | MBT/Most Supported | 1 | Conditional Diagramming; Causal Chains; Science | Easy inference built on a causal/conditional chain. States the general move ("if X leads to Y and Y leads to Z, infer X leads to Z"), predicts the chained answer, and rejects B as a sufficient condition misread as necessary and E as reversed causation. | The chain diagram is inline text and loses its layout. |\r\n| 2 | PT145-S2-Q23 | MBT/Most Supported | 2 | Sufficient vs. Necessary Flaw; Contrapositive Logic; Quantifiers | Explains which statement commands attention (a lone Most claim is inert, the conditional is not), contraposes, and diagrams the inference before the choices. Wrong answers are refuted with concrete counterexample numbers (100 Velyena mines vs 11 gold mines), the clearest quantifier teaching in the corpus. | **REFERENCE ONLY (Codex review, 2026-09-27):** the correct-answer note reverses the predicates twice ("have violated" for "have never violated", and "If have violated, then not gold" for "If gold, then have violated"). Borrow the counterexample numbers, never the note. |\r\n| 3 | PT104-S1-Q3 | MBT/Most Supported | 3 | Explain Curious Fact; Important Pivot; Alternatives; Causal Difference-Maker; Rebuttals | Inference that ends on a rebuttal: frames the two competing causal explanations (size vs age) and predicts a safely worded answer that holds age constant. B and C are shown to misplace which variable is held constant. | Goal step spends a paragraph on facts vs opinions before the prediction. |\r\nArgument Structure WordsCONC: so, thus, hence, therefore, consequently, this showsthat, because of this, this demonstrates, concludeEVIDENCE: for, after all, because, since (F.A.B.S.)AUTHOR: but, yet, however, should, clearly, obvi, probablyCONCESSION: although, while, despite, even though\r\nConditional Logic: Universals, Guarantees, Requirements\r\nGIVEN A\r\n→ B X\r\n→ ~Y and ZCONTRAPOSITIVE: ~B\r\n→ ~A Y or ~Z\r\n→ ~X\r\nLEFT SIDE: if, when, the only, any, each, every, allRIGHT SIDE: only if, only, ensures, guarantees, always,depends on, requires, must, then ( Req’d = Right side )IF NOT: unless, without, until (negate what goes on Left)BICONDITIONAL: if and only if, then and only thenNEITHER/NOR: “not this and not that”NESTED UNLESS: “If then A, then B, unless C”= A + ~C –> B\r\nFamous FlawsNecessary vs. Sufficient: reverses or negates a conditionalPart vs. Whole: assumes a trait/property applies to bothCausal: too sure of one possible explanation for somethingUnproven vs. Proven False: no ev / bad arg for X\r\n≠ X falseSampling: sample is biased / too-small / unrepresentativeAd Hominem: dismiss view bc source is biased / hypocriticalInappropriate Appeals: emotion / opinion / dubious expertFalse Choice: treats 2 like the only 2 / assumes exclusivityIntent vs. Result: acts like all consequences are intendedEquivocation: a word or concept is used two very diff waysCircular: premise restates or assumes the conclusionSelf Contradiction: two of author’s claims butt heads\r\nQuantity TermsAt least one is = some, can, may, might, possible, couldAt least one isn’t = not all, not required, not sufficientAt least a handful = many, often, commonlyMore than 50% = most, typically, usually, generally, tendsto, likely, probably, few fail to be, almost all, nearly all\r\nNegative Translations: No A’s are B = All A’s are ~BFew A’s are B = Most A’s are ~BNot all A’s are B’s = Some A’s are ~B’s\r\nInferences with “Most” StatementsMost A’s are B Most A’s are B Most A’s are B+ Most A’s are C + Most B’s are ~A + Most B’s are C\r\nSome B’s are C More B’s than A’s No inference\r\n***ASSUMPTION / OBJECTION FAMILY***\r\nIDing Assumptions: Conc has a new idea the Evid didn’t\r\nIDing Objections: Given the Evid is true, how could I stillargue the Conc is wrong? (how can I argue the Anti-Conc?)\r\nExplain Curious Fact: (common on Str / Wkn / Flaw / NA)Evidence describes a curious comparison, often a CorrelationConclusion proposes a Causal Explanation- Think: Are there any Alternate Explanations?Reverse Causality? Both Effects of the same Cause?\r\n- How Plausible is the Author\'s Explanation?Cause w/o Eff? Eff w/o Cause? No Cause, No Eff (control group)?\r\nFlaw (Very Predictable. 40% test a Famous Flaw)If no Famous Flaw, ID an Assumption or an Objection- 3 types of answers: Assumption, Objection, DescriptionASSUMPTION: takes for granted, presumes, fails to establish- beware: strong/broad, use negation test (see below) if unsure\r\nOBJECTION: fails to consider, neglects/overlooks possibility- if the overlooked thing was true, would it weaken the argument?\r\nDESCRIPTION: everything else- Did the arg really do this? If so, is that really a problem?- Infers from (evid) that (conc); Treats (evid) as though (conc)\r\nNecessary Assumption (Less Predictable, esp Q15-26)½ test a Missing Link Assumption, ½ Rule Out an Objection- Don’t see a Missing Link? Use Anti-Conclusion to ID an Obj.- BEWARE strong/broad; EMBRACE weak, ruling-out, “not”- NEGATION TEST: which answer, if negated, most weakens\r\nSufficient Assumption (answer + evid\r\n→ conclusion)- “New Idea” in Conc connected to 1-mention Ev Idea- Correct answer often contrapositive (CP) of prediction- Wrong answers: right ideas, wrong order; lack new idea\r\nPrinciple-Strengthen (Very Predictable, Missing Link)- “If [facts from Evid], then [Judgment from Conc]” or CP.- Wrong Answers: different judgment, connect the judgmentto unknowns/opposites instead of the Evid facts, assign thejudgment to the wrong party (cops should vs juries should)or go in the wrong order (If judgment, then facts)\r\nStrengthen / Weaken / Evaluate (Less Predictable)- mainly Explain Curious Fact, Plans/Recommendations, orarguments by Comparison/Analogy.STR: Support Plausibility, Neutralize Objections, Add HelpWKN: Attack Plausibility/Assumptions, Raise ObjectionsEVAL: which question has the power to Str or Wkn?\r\n***MATCH FAMILY***\r\nParallel / Parallel Flaw (Predictable Structure, not Content)- If there are Conditional or Quantified claims, Diagram- If not, try to describe the logic abstractly (e.g. “rules outone explanation in order to prove another”)- Par Flaw: Name Flaw 1st! often Nec vs. Suff / Part vs. Whole- Correct: often scrambles order. Trap: similar topic / words\r\nMain Conclusion (Very Predictable)- 90% of them: First line or Pivot/Rebuttal (but/yet/however)- Traps: Last Claim, Intermediate Conclusion, Inference\r\nRole (Predict: Main Conc, Supports, Opposes, Neutral)- Like Main Conc, mostly “upside down args” (Conc above Ev)- Intermediate conclusions give support and have support.\r\nMethod (common moves: analogies/distinctions, faultyimplications, or eliminating/suggesting alternatives)\r\nPrinciple-Conform (Predictable Structure, not Content)Principle/s in the Stimulus: match like a Parallel Q.Principle in Answers: play like Principle Strengthen Q.\r\n***INFERENCE FAMILY***\r\nMust Be True / Most Supported (Very Predictable)- Find 2+ claims that can be combined. Look for OverlappingConcepts, conditional/causal wording, or pivots creatingtension. MBT favors Conditional and Mathy stuff. MSSfavors Causality and Comparisons.- BEWARE new ideas, EMBRACE weak/familiar ideas\r\nMust Be False (rare, Predictable Structure)Answer contradicts a provided conditional or an inference\r\nAgree / Disagree (Very Predictable)- Read both, then TAKE PERSON 1’s CLAIMS one at a time:“did person 2 (dis)agree with this claim or not address it?”- If you can’t find explicit overlap, consider assumptions.- BEWARE weak answer language. It’s hard to disagree with!\r\nParadox (Less Predictable, esp Q15-26)GIVEN the background fact(s), HOW CAN WE EXPLAIN thesurprising claim?- Usually Comparative: right answer is a helpful Difference- Traps contradict background facts or deepen the paradox.- BEWARE weak answers. EMBRACE strong/new ideas.\r\n'}]
