So there is no reason not to try my herb juice.\r\nSTEM: The reasoning in the herbalist\'s argument is flawed because the argument\r\n## Conclusion (So)\r\nThere is no reason not to try my herb juice.\r\n## Evidence\r\nCustomers report improved physical coordination.\r\n  A few doctors say the herbs are potentially harmful, but doctors are always trying to maintain a monopoly over medical therapies.\r\n## Evaluate\r\nIf we\'re the other lawyer and we\'re arguing,\r\n*there IS some reason not to try this herb juice*\r\nwhat would we say?\r\n  We could start with, "wait — some doctors said the juice was harmful? That would be a reason not to try it."\r\n  And the author will say, "Oh, don\'t listen to them. Doctors don\'t want herbs to work. They want a monopoly over medicine, so they talk smack about anything that isn\'t pharmaceuticals."\r\n  We need a response. We could say, "Sure, I would imagine that Big Pharma is incentivized to diminish non-pharmaceutical treatments. Buuuut, we can\'t just ignore warnings that something is harmful. What if it\'s actually harmful? Do you have evidence it\'s safe?"\r\n  If we know our Famous Flaws, then we\'ll hopefully recognize this as a classic Ad Hominem. That\'s when the author dismisses someone\'s view because they (1) have an ulterior motive / vested interest, or (2) have conflicting past behavior / hypocrisy.\r\n  In this case, the author is trying to immediately disqualify what these doctors are saying just because doctors do have a financial interest in herbal treatments being bad ideas.\r\n## Goal\r\nLook for an answer addressing the Ad Hominem move where the author fails to seriously address the concerns of the doctors and wipes them away by pointing out their self-interest.\r\n  Or, there might be an answer saying, "even if the herb juice isn\'t harmful, there still might be some *other* reason not to try it" (e.g. its awful taste, the farts it gives you, etc.)\r\n===== PT150-S2-Q4 Flaw key A\r\nSTIM: When politicians describe their opponents\' positions, they typically make those positions seem implausible and unattractive. In contrast, scholars try to make opposing positions seem as plausible and attractive as possible. Doing so makes their arguments against those positions more persuasive to their professional colleagues. Politicians should take note: they could persuade more voters with their arguments if they simply followed the scholars in charitably formulating their opponents\' positions.\r\nSTEM: The reasoning in the argument is most vulnerable to criticism on the grounds that it\r\n## Conclusion (should = opinion)\r\nPoliticians could persuade more voters with their arguments if they simply followed the scholars in charitably formulating their opponents\' positions.\r\n## Evidence\r\nWhen scholars try to make an opposing position seem as plausible and attractive as possible (when they formulate it as charitably as possible), it makes their argument more persuasive to their colleagues.\r\n## Evaluate\r\nSince this argument is based on a Comparison, we try to think of salient differences in order to object that the comparison is a shaky one.\r\n  For example, suppose we said, "Marathon runners never run a full marathon as a practice run because it\'s too depleting. LSAT students should take note: they could fare better if they simply followed the marathon runners\' example and didn\'t take a full practice test."\r\n  We could think of a salient difference: wait a sec, even though taking a full-length LSAT practice is depleting, the recovery time is much faster than for a runner who runs a 26-mile practice run.\r\n  So when it comes to politicians and scholars .... can we think about any salient difference for why the advice of "portray your opponents\' arguments as persuasively as possible" would hold for the latter but not for the former?\r\n## Goal\r\nLook for an answer possibly saying that even though presenting opponents\' arguments charitably makes scholars look good to their colleagues, it still isn\'t a good idea for politicians to do (since it would make their political rivals seem more level-headed and worth listening to, which is usually the opposite of a politician\'s objectives).\r\n-- A: When we see Flaw answers start with fails to consider / ignores the possibility, we treat them like Weaken answer choices and ask ourselves whether we could object to the argument using this idea.\r\n\r\nWould it hurt this argument if we said, "Hey, author -- yes, that approach (presenting opponents\' views charitably) works with one kind of audience (fellow scholars), but it will not work well with another (a politician\'s audience)?"\r\n\r\nSure, that sounds like an objection. It\'s essentially just punching back at the author\'s suggestion that was works well for scholars will also work well for politicians.\r\n\r\nWe can imagine that people at a rally for Bernie Sanders might not respond well if Bernie presents Ted Cruz\'s arguments super charitably, and people at a rally for Ted Cruz might not respond well if Ted presents Bernie\'s arguments very charitably.\r\n-- B: When we see Flaw answers start with fails to consider / ignores the possibility, we can ask ourselves whether we could object to the argument using this idea.\r\n\r\nCould we say, "Hey, author -- I know you\'re recommending that politicians express their opponents\' positions very charitably, but it\'s difficult to come up with charitable formulations of opposing positions"?\r\n\r\nNot really. The author never promised it would be easy. She only said that, "if a politician charitably formulated her opponents\' positions, then she could persuade more voters with her arguments".\r\n\r\nThis answer would be objecting to the If-condition, which is not something we ever do. We can only object to this conclusion by describing a world in which politicians do succeed in charitably formulating their opponents\' positions, but it does not persuade more voters to agree with their arguments.\r\n-- C: The author does establish a difference between the politician\'s style of argumentation (make opponent\'s position seem implausible and unattractive) and the scholar\'s style (make opposing position seem as plausible and attractive as possible).\r\n\r\nThe second half of this answer is complaining that the author "focused on different styles even though those two styles might be suited to similar audiences".\r\n\r\nThat complaint doesn\'t make any sense. Are we trying to say that scholars and politicians have similar audiences? That doesn\'t sound like our complaint. It sounds more like the opposite of our complaint. The way a scholar talks to other scholars seems like it could play be vastly different rules than the way a politician talks to voters.\r\n\r\nSo this answer would be closer to being okay if said, "even though those styles might be suited to different audiences". That would allow us to complain, "Yes, author, they have two very different styles of argumentation -- but that\'s because they have to very different audiences, so it\'s appropriate for the styles to be different".\r\n-- D: When we see Flaw answers start with takes for granted / presumes, we treat them like Necessary Assumption answer choices and ask ourselves whether the author needed to assume this idea.\r\n\r\nWas this author assuming that "both scholars and politicians have persuasion as their aim"?\r\n\r\nWell, we could say, "No, the author is only thinking that both scholars and politicians would be more persuasive if they presented opposing positions charitably." An author could conclude, "If you wear cologne, you will be more attractive to others", and that isn\'t assuming that "being more attractive to others is a goal we have. It\'s just speaking to cause and effect.\r\n\r\nWe could also say, "sure, the author is assuming that both scholars and politicians have persuasion as one of their aims." But we still wouldn\'t say that our biggest logical complaint with this author is that she\'s assuming that both scholars and politicians want to persuade. Our problem is that the author is assuming that because a technique has a certain effect for scholars, it will have a similar effect for politicians.\r\n-- E: When we see Flaw answers start with takes for granted / presumes, we treat them like Necessary Assumption answer choices and ask ourselves whether the author needed to assume this idea.\r\n\r\nWas this author assuming that "politicians formulate the positions of opponents uncharitably even when they share the same position?"\r\n\r\nNo, the author said that politicians typically make opponents\' positions seem implausible, not that they always do. The author doesn\'t need to assume that politicians frame an opponent\'s position unfairly even when they hold the same position.\r\n===== PT154-S1-Q12 Flaw key C\r\nSTIM: Art history professor: Costa criticizes my theories about the distinction between baroque and neoclassical Austrian painting. He argues that since there are no features possessed by all and only the works from a given historical period, assigning works of art to period styles is intellectually bankrupt. His reasoning can be discounted, however, since his own current theories on the transition from classical to romantic French opera also presuppose such an assignment.\r\nSTEM: Which one of the following most accurately describes a flaw in the art history professor\'s argument?\r\n## Conclusion\r\nCosta\'s reasoning can be discounted.\r\n## Evidence (since)\r\nHis own current theories presuppose the same assignment he\'s criticizing me for.\r\n## Evaluate\r\nWe could get into the details of Costa said, but they are immaterial to recognizing the Famous Flaw that\'s happening here.\r\nWhenever we\'re doing Flaw and we see a Rebuttal style argument, we know to look for these two of the 10 Famous Flaws:\r\n1. Ad Hominem — in which an author dismisses the view of person X because person X has some biased interest in the matter or because person X has come conflicting past behavior.\r\n2. Unproven vs. Proven False — in which an author concludes that X is false, since there is a lack of evidence that X is true or since someone\'s attempt to prove that X is true was a flawed attempt.\r\nThe conclusion here isn\'t saying that Costa is wrong, so we\'re not dealing with #2. It\'s saying Costa\'s view can be discounted / dismissed / disregarded, which is more the style of #1.\r\nWhy are we discounting Costa\'s critique? Because "Costa does the same thing". This is a little like getting busted by Mom for playing with matches and trying to argue that we don\'t deserve punishment because "Timmy did the same thing!"\r\nMom will say, "Okay, then both you and Timmy did something wrong."\r\nSimilarly, we could say to this professor, "Both you and Costa deserve to be criticized for trying to assign works of art to period styles."\r\nSomeone who commits tax fraud should still be listened to when they accuse person X of committing tax fraud. Someone who had their license suspended for a DUI should still be listened to when they tell person X that X has had too much to drink to get behind the wheel.\r\n## Goal\r\nLook for something saying, "Just because Costa is guilty of X doesn\'t mean we should discount Costa when he argues that we are guilty of X".\r\nThe answer can also say that the author fails to address the substantive point Costa makes, distracting us instead with irrelevant details about what Costa himself does in his theories.\r\n-- A: This answer describes the #1 famous flaw Necessary vs. Sufficient, in which the author presents a conditional logic premise and then illegally applies that rule in a backwards or negated fashion.\r\n\r\nThere\'s no conditional logic premise here. The only premise is "Costa\'s own theories presuppose such an assignment".\r\n-- B: Since this answer starts with fails to consider / overlooks the possibility, we can ask ourselves whether the idea that follows would Weaken.\r\n\r\nCan we hurt the professor\'s argument by telling her that "theoreticians can hold radically different theories at different times"?\r\n\r\nNo, that doesn\'t seem to be a relevant objection. Her premise is just about Costa\'s current theories, so who cares what different theories he (or other theoreticians) held at different times.\r\n-- C: We could understand this answer to be a "conflicting behavior" version of the famous Ad Hominem flaw: "My dad tells me not to smoke cigarettes because it will mess up my lungs. His reasoning can be discounted, however, since he smokes cigarettes". Obviously, Dad could be right about his advice and wrong about his behavior (do as I say, not as I do).\r\n\r\nWe could otherwise understand this answer by reacting to its structure:\r\nthe argument does X merely on the grounds that Y\r\n\r\nThis means that X should match the conclusion and Y should match the evidence. Was the conclusion "rejecting the reasoning on which a criticism is based"?\r\n\r\nYes: Costa\'s reasoning should be discounted was our conclusion, and Costa\'s reasoning was being used to criticize the professor\'s theories.\r\n\r\nDoes the evidence say that "that same criticism could be applied to the theories of the person who offered the criticism"?\r\n\r\nYes! Costa said that it was intellectually bankrupt to assign works of art to period styles, but the author\'s premise (since) is saying that Costa\'s own theories presuppose an assignment of works of art to period styles.\r\n-- D: This answer describes the famous flaw Part vs. Whole, in which the author assumes that a trait that belongs to the Whole is therefore possessed by each Part of the whole (or vice versa).\r\n\r\nIf an answer says "presumes that what is true of X must also be true of Y", then we want to see if X matches the evidence and Y matches the conclusion.\r\n\r\nDid the premise talk about "what is true of art in general"?\r\n\r\nHeavens, no. The premise says "Costa\'s own current theories presuppose the same assignment of works of art to period styles". We can stop reading here.\r\n\r\nThe conclusion also does not match; it is not a claim about "every particular style of art". It\'s the claim that "Costa\'s reasoning can be discounted".\r\n-- E: Since this answer starts with presumes / takes for granted, we can ask ourselves whether the idea that follows would be a Necessary Assumption.\r\n\r\nWas the author assuming that "theories about one type of art cannot be compared to theories about another"?\r\n\r\nNope. That\'s a crazy-extreme claim, and our author isn\'t saying anything related to that. She\'s only talking about Costa\'s theories. Also, to the extent that the author is saying that Costa\'s theories are guilty of the same "sin" that Costa accuses the author\'s theories of, the author seems to think that at least some theories can be compared to each other.\r\n===== PT113-S2-Q3 Flaw key B\r\nSTIM: Joan got A’s on all her homework assignments, so if she had gotten an A on her term paper, she could pass the course even without doing the class presentation. Unfortunately, she did not get an A on her term paper, so it is obvious that she will have to do the class presentation to pass the course.\r\nSTEM: The argument\'s reasoning is questionable because the argument\r\n## Conclusion (so it is obvious that)\r\nJoan will have to do the class presentation to pass the course.\r\n## Evidence\r\nIf Joan had gotten an A on her term paper, she could pass the course without doing the class presentation.\r\nBut she didn\'t get an A on her term paper.\r\n## Evaluate\r\nWhenever we\'re doing Flaw and we see any Conditional Logic, we should immediately be suspicious that the argument is going to commit an illegal negation or reversal (aka, the #1 Famous Flaw, **Necessary vs. Sufficient** ).\r\nThe first sentence has a conditional rule, signified by **if**.\r\nIf A on term paper, then pass without presentation.\r\nSo an illegal reversal would sound like, "Joan passed the course without doing the class presentation. Thus, she must have gotten an A on her term paper."\r\nAn illegal negation would sound like, "Joan didn\'t get an A on her term paper, so she can\'t pass without first doing the presentation."\r\nAnd that second thing is exactly what the argument did!\r\n## Goal\r\nWe can describe this flaw formally as confusing Necessary and Sufficient conditions. We were told in the evidence that getting an A on her term paper would have been **sufficient** to get Joan out of having to do the class presentation.\r\nBut the author is acting like it\'s **necessary**. He\'s acting like if she didn\'t get the A then she must do the class presentation.\r\nWe can describe the flaw conversationally by saying, "Fails to consider that there could be other things Joan potentially could have done to pass the course without doing the class presentation" (a doctor\'s note / extra credit / a bribe or threat from her parents / etc.)\r\n-- A: This author **takes for granted** / **presumes** that Joan has to either get an A on her term paper or do the class presentation. So this answer would be correct if it began with any of the assumption prefixes.\r\n\r\nIt doesn\'t **fail to consider / ignore the possibility** that she has to get an A or do the class presentation.\r\n\r\nThe former are prefixes that indicate what the author was thinking or assuming. The latter are prefixes that raise a potential objection to the argument. When we see fails to consider / overlooks the possibility that X, we ask ourselves if X would weaken.\r\n\r\nWould it hurt the argument if we said, "Hey, author -- Joan has to either get an A on her term paper or do the class presentation to pass the course"?\r\n\r\nDefinitely not. That completely matches the author\'s thinking.\r\n-- B: Since this answer starts with takes for granted / presumes / presupposes that X, we can just ask ourselves, "Was the author assuming X?"\r\n\r\nDid this author assume that Joan\'s not getting an A on the term paper prevents her from passing the course without doing the class presentation?\r\n\r\nYes! That perfectly matches the reasoning move. The author establishes that Joan didn\'t get an A, and then moves from that idea to the conclusion that she will have to do the class presentation in order to pass the course.\r\n\r\nIf we negated this assumption (not getting an A **doesn\'t** prevent Joan from passing without doing the class presentation), would that weaken?\r\n\r\nTotally! This author thinks she has to do the class presentation in order to pass, but this negation would be saying the opposite.\r\n\r\nWhy is it bad for the author to be assuming that not getting an A prevents Joan from passing the course without doing the class presentation?\r\n\r\nBecause we were never told that passing the course w/o doing the class presentation was dependent on getting an A.\r\n\r\nWe know that getting an A is **one potential way** that you can pass the course without doing the class presentation (sufficient). But we were never told it\'s the **only way** to pass the course without doing the class presentation (necessary), as the author seems to be thinking.\r\n-- C: Nothing in the argument has anything to do with her overall course grade, so this feels out of scope.\r\n\r\nSince the answer begins with fails to consider / overlooks the possibility that X, we want to ask ourselves if X could weaken.\r\n\r\nHere, X isn\'t expressed as a clause but rather as a long phrase. Still we could turn it into a clause.\r\n\r\nWe can ask ourselves, "Would it hurt the author\'s argument if we said Hey, author -- class presentations are very important to a student\'s overall grade".\r\n\r\nNo, that wouldn\'t hurt the argument at all. The author was never talking about overall grade nor was she assuming anything about whether class presentations have or don\'t have an important connection to overall grade.\r\n-- D: Like (A), this answer is pairing up something the author thinks with the prefix fails to consider / ignores the possibility.\r\n\r\nThe author\'s premise says, If get A on term paper, then don\'t need to do CP\r\n\r\nby contrapositive that means, if do need to do CP, then didn\'t get A on term paper\r\n\r\nThose two things mean the same thing. A conditional and its contrapositive have the same meaning, just like "X is bigger than Y" has the same meaning as "Y is smaller than X".\r\n\r\nSo the author\'s premise said "X > Y" and this answer is saying the author ignored the possibility that "Y < X". The author announced that possibility; she didn\'t ignore it.\r\n-- E: Since this answer starts with fails to consider / ignores the possibility that X, we can just ask ourselves, "Does X weaken?"\r\n\r\nCan we object to this argument by saying, "Yo, author -- some students get A\'s on their term paper but don\'t pass the course"?\r\n\r\nNope. The author\'s argument is about someone who **didn\'t** get an A on their term paper, so whatever is true of people who **do** get an A on their term paper is irrelevant to this conversation about Joan\'s situation.\r\n'}]

Well, it\'s an incredibly shallow source of evidence, double entendré intended. It\'s shallow as in way too small a sample. The conclusion is causal (\'body size influences mating decisions\'), but we can\'t really call \'body size\' an event.\\r\\nAnd we didn\'t find ourselves yelling at this author, \\"Hey, man, maybe both body size and mating decisions are being caused by some 3rd factor.\\"\\r\\n-- B: This answer describes a variation of the famous Sampling flaw, in which an author\'s conclusion is a generalization / extrapolation, and the evidence is a sample that we criticize for being too small, an unrepresentative slice of the population, a self-selecting sample, or at high risk of having dishonest responses.\\r\\nThe sample of \\"college students and personal ads\\" is likely to be unrepresentative of \\"mating decisions throughout all societies\\".\\r\\nAsking the co-eds at Arizona State or the users of Tinder how they make their mating decisions and expecting that their answers will be the same as if we asked a remote hunter-gather society in Ethiopia seems like a stretch.\\r\\nSo the author should not be generalizing about all societies based on this incredibly narrow subset of data points.\\r\\n-- C: Could we fairly say that the author concludes that \\"an effect has only one cause\\"?\\r\\nNo. The conclusion says \'body size influences mating decisions\', which doesn\'t mean it\'s the only cause of mating decisions.\\r\\n-- D: Does the author draw a conclusion about individual persons?\\r\\nNo. The conclusion says \'body size influences mating decisions throughout all societies\'.\\r\\n-- E: It\'s pretty freaky that they would put another answer choice referencing the Sampling flaw.\\r\\nIt does seem like the evidence is too small, given that the conclusion is about all the societies of the world. No matter how accurate a picture we have about the mating decisions of college students and people who place personal ads, we still won\'t feel comfortable assuming that their mating decisions are akin to those of all other people.\\r\\n===== PT124-S2-Q11 Flaw key D\\r\\nSTIM: In order to maintain a high standard of living, a nation must maintain a functioning infrastructure. Major investment in the improvement of its infrastructure will, over time, reward a nation with a corresponding rise in its standard of living. Hence a nation whose standard of living is on the rise can be safely assumed to be a nation that has invested heavily in improving its infrastructure.\\r\\nSTEM: The reasoning in the argument is flawed because the argument fails to take into account that\\r\\n## Conclusion (Hence)\\r\\nA nation whose standard of living is on the rise can be safely assumed to be a nation that has invested heavily in improving its infrastructure.\\r\\n## Evidence\\r\\nMaintaining a high standard of living requires maintaining a functioning infrastructure.\\r\\nMajor investment in improving infrastructure will lead to a nation with a corresponding rise in standard of living.\\r\\n## Evaluate\\r\\nWhenever we\'re doing Flaw and we see any conditional logic in the premises, we immediately start assuming we\'ll going to probably see a Necessary vs. Sufficient flaw (where the author supplies a conditional relationship or two in the evidence, but then applies that rule in some backwards or opposite manner to reach the conclusion).\\r\\nThe first sentence has \\"in order to X, you must Y\\". The second has \\"doing A will lead to B\\".\\r\\nThe conclusion is also a pseudo-conditional, because it\'s saying \\"something that is P can be assumed to be Q\\".\\r\\nSo we might need to diagram these if we\'re trying to figure out exactly where the author might be making an illegal reversal or negation.\\r\\nPrem 1: maintain high S.O.L → maintain functioning Inf\\r\\nPrem 2: major invest in improving Inf → rise in S.O.L\\r\\nDo those two premises chain together? No, not really. To chain, you need an overlapping term. \\"Standard of living\\" is an overlapping term, but we\'re talking about it in two different ways -- whether or not you\'re maintaining a **high** standard of living / whether or not your standard of living has risen **higher** than it previously was.\\r\\nIf those were the same we could make this chain: major invest → high S.O.L → maintain in impr Inf. funct. Inf\\r\\nBut they\'re not. You could raise your standard of living from 150th in the world to 140th. It would be **higher** but it wouldn\'t be a **high** standard of living that you\'re maintaining.\\r\\nOkay, so the conditional premises don\'t chain together. So is there a conditional logic problem with the conclusion? Yes. The conclusion looks like this: S.O.L is rising → Invested heavily in imp Inf\\r\\nThat\'s an illegal reversal of the 2nd sentence: invest heavily in imp Inf → rise in S.O.L.\\r\\nWe were told that major investments would lead to a rise in S.O.L.\\r\\nBut other things might also lead to a rise in S.O.L (better daycare and family leave options / cleaner environments / industrial ingenuity creating new jobs and wealth like in Silicon Valley).\\r\\nIf a country\'s standard of living is on the rise, we can\'t assume it\'s because of major infrastructure investment. It might be because of one of these other things that cause standard of living to rise.\\r\\n## Goal\\r\\nLook for an answer complaining about the illegal backwards conditional move (\\"fails to consider that even though major investment is a sufficient condition that guarantees a rise in standard of living, it\'s not necessarily accompanying every rise in standard of living\\").\\r\\nOr look for an answer making the more conversational objection that \\"the argument fails to consider that a nation whose standard of living is on the rise might be in that position for some reason other than having heavily improved its infrastructure\\".\\r\\n-- A: Saying, \\"Something that is X need not be Y\\" is an objection to an author who said, \\"Bob is X. Thus, Bob must be Y.\\"\\r\\n\\r\\nThis author didn\'t argue that \\"if you\'ve failed to invest in infrastructure then you must be experiencing **a decline** in standard of living\\". He is thinking that, \\"if you failed to invest in infrastructure, then you would **not have a rising** standard of living.\\"\\r\\n\\r\\n\\"Not having a rising standard of living\\" isn\'t the same as having a declining standard of living. A country might just have a steady standard of living.\\r\\n-- B: Saying, \\"many X\'s are unable to Y\\" would only be an objection to an author who thinks, \\"almost all X\'s are able to Y\\".\\r\\n\\r\\nDid this author think that \\"almost all nations are able to make needed investments in infrastructure\\"?\\r\\n\\r\\nNope. None of the conversation is about whether nations are **able** to make investments. The author isn\'t announcing whether nations are commonly or rarely able to make such investments. She\'s only claiming that when you see a nation with a rising standard of living, then you can assume that that nation was able to invest heavily.\\r\\n-- C: The author concedes the potential for a gradual timeline by saying, \\"Major investment will, **over time**, reward a nation with a rise in standard of living.\\"\\r\\n-- D: Saying, \\"X need not be the result of Y\\" would be an objection to an author who thinks \\"when X happens, it must be because of Y\\".\\r\\n\\r\\nDid this author think that when \\"rise in standard of living\\" happens, it must be because of \\"major investments in infrastructure\\"?\\r\\n\\r\\nYup! That\'s basically what the conclusion says: \\"If a rise in standard of living happens, then you can safely assume that major investment in infrastructure happened.\\"\\r\\n\\r\\nThis answer is pointing out the objection we made that a nation\'s standard of living could be on the rise for many, many reasons. Maybe it\'s infrastructure investment. Maybe it\'s environmental cleanup. Maybe it\'s a boost to the social welfare offered.\\r\\n\\r\\nHence, seeing a nation with a rising standard of living wouldn\'t allow us to assume that this was a result of infrastructure investment.\\r\\n-- E: Saying, \\"X\'s often have to do Y\\" would only be an objection to an author who was thinking \\"X\'s almost never have to do Y\\".\\r\\n\\r\\nWas our author thinking that \\"nations **almost never** experience short-term crises that require diverting resources away from infrastructure improvements\\"?\\r\\n\\r\\nHeck no. That\'s getting extremely specific about a lot of stuff the author never even talked about. This answer feels a lot like (B). It\'s trying to object to the author by saying, \\"Hey, author -- sometimes nations aren\'t **able** to invest in infrastructure.\\"\\r\\n\\r\\nCool. He never said they were. He\'s only saying if you see a nation with a rising standard of living, then you know that that nation was able to invest in infrastructure.\\r\\n===== PT155-S1-Q20 Flaw key B\\r\\nSTIM: Mr. Klemke argues that the complaints recently lodged against his roofing company are unfounded, on the grounds that each of the complainants disagrees with Klemke\'s widely known political views and is therefore biased. However, having a different political outlook from Klemke\'s would in no way prevent one from being badly treated by his company. Clearly, then, the complaints are not, as Mr. Klemke alleges, unfounded.\\r\\nSTEM: The argument against Mr. Klemke\'s allegation is flawed in that it\\r\\n## Conclusion (Clearly, then)\\r\\nThe complaints are not unfounded.\\r\\n  rephrase: There is legit reason to believe the complaints.\\r\\n## Evidence\\r\\nKlemke says that the complainants disagree with his widely known political views and they are therefore biased.\\r\\n  But having different politics is still compatible with being badly treated by his company (different politics in no way prevents you from being badly treated).\\r\\n## Evaluate\\r\\nWhenever we\'re doing Flaw and we see a Rebuttal style argument, we want to look for the two Top 10 Famous Flaws that deal with rebuttals:\\r\\n  1. Ad Hominem (dismissing someone\'s view because the source is biased or because the source has conflicting past behavior)\\r\\n  2. Unproven vs. Proven False (explaining why one person\'s attempt to prove X fails, but then illicitly concluding that this shows that X is false)\\r\\n  #1 is actually what Klemke is doing. He\'s committing an Ad Hominem flaw by dismissing the views of the complainants simply because they are a biased source (they don\'t like his politics).\\r\\n  Our author points out to Klemke that this is a flawed argument. You can\'t ignore complaints about your roofing company just because the source of the complaints is biased. That\'s a flawed / inadequate argument.\\r\\n  But #2 does seem to be what the author is doing. The easiest way to spot Unproven vs. Proven False is to notice that the author\'s conclusion is the complete opposite of the opponent\'s conclusion.\\r\\n  For example:\\r\\n  Sheila thinks 5G networks cause cancer, but the research paper she found to support that judgment turns out to be forged by a conspiracy theorist. Therefore, 5G networks do not cause cancer.\\r\\n  The correct, responsible conclusion to draw when someone makes an unsuccessful argument in favor of X is that, \\"We don\'t currently know whether X is true.\\" After all, how does this author know that 5G networks don\'t cause cancer? Does he have evidence to support that claim?\\r\\n## Goal\\r\\nIn this argument, Klemke was alleging that \\"these complaints are unfounded\\". The author explains to us why Klemke\'s support for that allegation was flawed and inadequate.\\r\\n  But the author isn\'t allowed to go to the opposite side of the spectrum and say, \\"Clearly, then the complaints are well founded\\".\\r\\n  You can\'t say, \\"The committee didn\'t find criminal wrongdoing in Patrick\'s dealings with Russia. Therefore, Patrick had no criminal wrongdoing in his dealings with Russia.\\"\\r\\n  Let\'s look for something describing the illegal Unproven vs. Proven False move, or pointing out that even though Klemke\'s reason for rejecting the complaints was a bogus one, there still might be a legitimate reason to reject the complaints.\\r\\n-- A: This answer is saying that our author took the consequence/effect of something to be the cause of something.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe nickname for that concept is Reverse Causality. An author might see a correlation between X and Y and conclude that X causes Y, while failing to consider that Y really caused X. In such an argument, we could say that the author \\"took a consequence of Y to be a cause of Y\\".\\r\\n\\r\\nBut nothing in this argument has anything to do with causality, so this does not match anything.\\r\\n-- B: This describes the famous Unproven vs. Proven False flaw.\\r\\n\\r\\nOn earlier LSAT\'s, this flaw was easier to spot:\\r\\n\\"No one has ever proven that cell phones cause cancer. Thus, cell phones don\'t cause cancer\\".\\r\\n\\r\\nAnd the answer would say \\"Confuses an absence of evidence with evidence of absence\\".\\r\\n\\r\\nBut on more modern tests, instead of saying \\"there\'s a lack of evidence for X\\", they like to do, \\"Someone made a crappy argument in favor of X\\".\\r\\n\\r\\nTheir argument for X rested on a dubious assumption, or in this case made some illegal Ad Hominem move.\\r\\n\\r\\nBut just because their reasoning was too faulty to prove X doesn\'t mean we have any grounds for assuming X is false.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf we weren\'t familiar with this Top 10 famous flaw, then we would simply look at the form of this answer, Concludes X on the grounds that Y, and try to match X to the conclusion and Y to the evidence.\\r\\n\\r\\nDid the author conclude that a claim is false?\\r\\n\\r\\nYes, \\"Mr. Klemke\'s allegation that the complaints are unfounded is false.\\"\\r\\n\\r\\nWas the evidence talking about an inadequate argument being offered for the claim that the complaints are unfounded?\\r\\n\\r\\nYes, it told us about how Klemke unsuccessfully tried to prove that the complaints were unfounded by pointing to political bias on the part of the complainants.\\r\\n\\r\\nSince everything matches, and since it describes an objectionable move, we can pick it.\\r\\n-- C: This answer describes the top 10 famous flaw, Ad Hominem, in which an author dismisses a point of view because the source is biased or has conflicting past behavior.\\r\\n\\r\\nOur author is trying to dismiss Klemke\'s view. Does she do so by citing a premise that \\"Klemke is biased?\\"\\r\\n\\r\\nNo. Klemke committed this flaw in how he reasoned about the complainants. Our author didn\'t commit this flaw.\\r\\n-- D: This refers to the top 10 famous flaw Sampling, in which an argument relies on a sample of data points that we have reason to believe is too small, self-selecting, unrepresentative, biased, or otherwise problematic.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis argument doesn\'t rely on a sample at all. Our author attempts to prove that Klemke\'s allegation is wrong by discussing Klemke\'s flawed way of dismissing the complaints.\\r\\n-- E: When a Flaw answer choice starts with fails to consider / overlooks the possibility, we can ask ourselves whether the idea that follows would potentially weaken the argument.\\r\\n\\r\\nCould we say, \\"Hey, author — the complaints are unfounded. After all, people whose views diverge are unaware of their disagreement.\\"\\r\\n\\r\\nThat doesn\'t feel like it makes any sense.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe complainants have view that widely diverge from Klemke\'s political views. Do we know or care whether the complainants are aware of that disagreement?\\r\\n\\r\\nWe don\'t know and we don\'t care. That has more to do with Klemke\'s argument, whereas we\'re here to critique this author who is saying, \\"Since Klemke\'s reason for dismissing the complaints as unfounded was a dumb reason, the complaints must be well founded.\\"\\r\\n"}'}]

## Tool: exec

const patch="*** Begin Patch\n*** Add File: C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/corpus/lr-explanations/drafts/batch-01-flaw-part-a.md\n+---\n+type: draft\n+subject: First 20 remaining Flaw slots, batch 01 part A\n+status: draft, not loaded\n+created: 2026-09-28\n+---\n+\n+# Flaw explanations: first 20 remaining slots\n+\n+Source: current designated bank (`projects/lsatu-wt-glossary/public/data`) and fresh database dump (`system/scripts/lsatlab-pull/data/db`). Bank and database keys agree for all 20. PT102-S3-Q14 has existing breakdown steps, so this draft supplies only its five missing notes. All other slots supply breakdown and five notes. No answer-choice disagreement identified in this subset. Model references are from the database; weaknesses noted in `models/Flaw.md` were not copied.\n+\n+## 1. PT101-S3-Q2 · Flaw · Difficulty 1 · Answer E\n+\n+*Modelled on PT150-S2-Q4 (comparison), PT107-S4-Q18 (test the abstract wording), and PT154-S2-Q12 (missing alternative).*\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Conclusion (thus)** Skin should get regular Dewyfresh moisturizer to protect it from damage caused by lack of moisture.\n+\n+**Evidence** Drought dries and cracks the earth, which the advertisement calls “the skin of the world.”\n+\n+**Evaluate** Given that dry ground cracks, how could skin still behave differently? Soil and human skin are different materials. The ad needs the two to resemble each other in the very process it is comparing. The phrase “skin of the world” is a metaphor, not evidence that a moisturizer affects skin the way rain affects soil. Even if skin needs moisture, nothing here establishes that Dewyfresh provides the promised protection.\n+\n+**Goal** Look for a criticism of an analogy between the earth and human skin that has not established the relevant similarity.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Wrong Flaw.** Necessary versus sufficient would require the ad to argue, for example, that because moisture is required for healthy skin, applying Dewyfresh guarantees beautiful skin. It does not make that formal move. Its evidence is the earth analogy.\n+\n+**(B) Wrong Flaw.** A common cause would explain why two observed events happen together. The ad does not report two correlated events and infer a shared cause. It compares two different things.\n+\n+**(C) Wrong Flaw.** The argument does not claim that changing beliefs changes physical reality. The opening addresses people who doubt moisturizers, but the evidence is about drought, not a change in what people believe.\n+\n+**(D) Wrong Flaw.** “Infusion” is used to mean an input of moisture in both places. The conclusion does not switch from a process to its product. The questionable switch is from ground to human skin.\n+\n+**(E) Correct.** Test the analogy's relevant feature. Dry soil cracks without water; does human skin respond to a moisturizer in the same way? The ad gives no reason to think so. Calling earth “skin” makes the analogy memorable but does not supply the biological similarity the conclusion needs.\n+\n+## 2. PT102-S2-Q18 · Flaw · Difficulty 3 · Answer E\n+\n+*Modelled on PT154-S2-Q12 (false choice), PT110-S2-Q11 (best versus only), and PT150-S2-Q4 (plans).*\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Conclusion (consequently)** The company's only options are to reduce planned expansion or eliminate some less profitable current operations.\n+\n+**Evidence** Demand and profit have fallen. Continued decline may cause bankruptcy, so preventing further profit decline matters.\n+\n+**Evaluate** Granted, the company needs to stop profits falling. Why must it choose one of those two cost cuts? It might increase revenue through a new product, revise prices, or find a third cost saving. The urgency of the goal does not prove an exhaustive list of ways to reach it. We need only one possible third way to challenge “only.”\n+\n+**Goal** Find the missing alternatives to stopping profit decline, not merely alternatives to bankruptcy.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Irrelevant Assumption.** Whether the company's survival is good does not show that the two listed measures exhaust its ways to prevent further loss.\n+\n+**(B) Wrong Target.** This sounds tempting because bankruptcy is mentioned. But the president's *only options* claim concerns stopping the decrease in profits. A way to survive after profits keep falling would not be a third way to stop that fall.\n+\n+**(C) Too Narrow.** The two listed options might lower costs, but the president has not said in general that only cost reduction can prevent profit losses. A third cost-saving measure would already expose the false choice. The flaw is the supposedly exhaustive list.\n+\n+**(D) Does Not Weaken.** Even a slight further decrease is a decrease. Its size does not show that there is another option for preventing it.\n+\n+**(E) Correct.** “Other ways to stop the decrease” is exactly the omitted third route. If another route exists, reducing expansion and eliminating operations are not the *only* options. This is the False Choice flaw.\n+\n+## 3. PT102-S3-Q14 · Flaw · Difficulty 1 · Answer C · Notes only\n+\n+*Existing Conclusion, Evidence, Evaluate, and Goal steps are retained. Modelled on PT154-S1-Q12 (ad hominem) and PT155-S1-Q20 (keeping the target speaker straight).*\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Wrong Flaw.** The herbalist does not warn customers of frightening consequences if they reject the juice. The doctors' warning that the herbs might be harmful is the point the herbalist dismisses.\n+\n+**(B) Wrong Flaw.** Customers can report improved coordination while the herbs still carry a risk of harm. Those claims can both be true, so the herbalist's evidence is not internally inconsistent.\n+\n+**(C) Correct.** The doctors assert that the herbs might be harmful. The herbalist replies that doctors want to protect a monopoly, then concludes there is no reason not to try the juice. A motive to make a claim does not show that the claim is false. The potential harm has not been addressed. This is the Ad Hominem move.\n+\n+**(D) Wrong Flaw.** The customer reports are not phrased to assume the juice is safe. The error occurs in dismissing the warning, not in wording the evidence as the conclusion.\n+\n+**(E) Tempting but wrong target.** The report that coordination improves *after* drinking juice might invite a post hoc objection if the conclusion were that the juice caused the improvement. The conclusion is that there is **no reason not to try** it. The live objection is the possible harm raised by doctors, and the herbalist attacks the doctors instead.\n+\n+## 4. PT102-S4-Q4 · Flaw · Difficulty 2 · Answer B\n+\n+*Modelled on PT154-S1-Q12 (ad hominem), PT155-S1-Q20 (separating a speaker from a claim), and PT154-S2-Q12 (testing abstract wording).*\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Conclusion** The proposed legislation must be defeated.\n+\n+**Evidence** Some supporters belong to groups that used violence in the past; other supporters face tax-evasion indictment.\n+\n+**Evaluate** Suppose every allegation about these supporters is true. What does that tell us about whether more paid vacation, higher overtime wages, or paid child care is a good law? Nothing by itself. A disreputable person can support a good proposal. The columnist offers no argument about the proposal's effects.\n+\n+**Goal** Look for the move from the character of the law's *supporters* to a verdict against the law.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Wrong People.** “Originators” means the people who proposed or created the legislation. The stimulus identifies people supporting it. We are not told who originated it.\n+\n+**(B) Correct.** The columnist assails the law because some *supporters* have questionable histories or face charges. That attacks the source of support rather than the legislation's merits. A charge is not even a conviction, but the flaw remains even if the character allegations are granted.\n+\n+**(C) Wrong Appeal.** The columnist names no people who would be adversely affected and makes no plea on their behalf. The appeal is against allegedly bad supporters.\n+\n+**(D) Unsupported Description.** The argument says nothing about the number of supporters inside or outside the legislature. It targets their character, not their count.\n+\n+**(E) Wrong Evidence.** The columnist never claims the supporters depend on manufacturers' success or act inconsistently. That would be a different attack on them.\n+\n+## 5. PT103-S1-Q5 · Flaw · Difficulty 3 · Answer A\n+\n+*Modelled on PT155-S1-Q20 (burden of proof in a rebuttal), PT103-S2-Q21 (absence of evidence), and PT154-S1-Q12 (matching the conclusion to the criticism).*\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Claim under dispute** The attorneys say the government destroyed evidence that would have helped the defendant.\n+\n+**Government's reply** There is no evidence that would even tend to help the defendant.\n+\n+**Evaluate** How could both statements be true? If the government destroyed the helpful evidence, none would remain now. “There is none” does not answer “Did you destroy it?” This is especially pointed because the alleged destruction would itself explain the absence the government cites.\n+\n+**Goal** Find the answer saying the reply leaves the destruction allegation unresolved.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Correct.** The reply reports an absence of helpful evidence but does not establish how that absence arose. Destruction remains possible. A denial of the current evidence's existence is not a denial of the alleged earlier destruction.\n+\n+**(B) Unsupported.** Calling the charge an exaggeration would require a reason to believe there was no destruction or that its scale was overstated. The reply provides neither.\n+\n+**(C) Unsupported.** The attorneys could have information about destroyed material. The government's statement tells us nothing about what the attorneys know.\n+\n+**(D) Unsupported.** There might be no evidence because a search found none, because none ever existed, or because it was destroyed. The reply alone does not prove a failure to search.\n+\n+**(E) Reverses the logic.** Even if the reply is true *now*, it does not disprove that helpful evidence existed and was destroyed. The alleged coverup is a possible explanation for the reply's truth.\n+\n+## 6. PT103-S2-Q1 · Flaw · Difficulty 1 · Answer B\n+\n+*Modelled on PT154-S1-Q12 (keep evidence aimed at the claim), PT155-S1-Q20 (rebuttal target), and PT150-S2-Q4 (identify the decisive difference).*\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Issue** Did the policy become effective before the attendant died? The local clock read 10 A.M., but that was before 9 A.M. in the purchase time zone.\n+\n+**Representative's conclusion** The insurer should pay the mother.\n+\n+**Evidence** The attendant was her sole support and she is ill.\n+\n+**Evaluate** Those facts make her loss especially hard. Do they settle what the policy's effective time means? No. The representative needs an argument about the contract's time zone or wording. A sympathetic beneficiary can still hold a policy that had not begun.\n+\n+**Goal** Find the appeal to pity in place of a response to the coverage issue.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Wrong Flaw.** The mother's illness and dependence are not a paraphrase of “the policy should pay.” They are distinct facts, albeit irrelevant to the contract timing.\n+\n+**(B) Correct.** Pity for the mother is understandable, but it does not answer the insurer's contention about when coverage began. The argument swaps an emotional reason to want payment for a reason the contract requires it.\n+\n+**(C) Wrong Distinction.** The representative makes no comparison between family obligations and business obligations. The defect is that family hardship does not determine the insurance contract.\n+\n+**(D) Wrong Target.** No person is attacked. The representative invites sympathy for the mother.\n+\n+**(E) Wrong Flaw.** No cause-and-effect relationship is reversed. The dispute is over the time at which a policy took effect.\n+\n+## 7. PT103-S2-Q4 · Flaw · Difficulty 2 · Answer C\n+\n+*Modelled on PT155-S1-Q20 (opponent's claim versus rebuttal), PT154-S1-Q12 (matching the target), and PT154-S2-Q12 (testing an alleged implication).*\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Opponent's claim** The government is obligated to raise taxes for schools and health care.\n+\n+**Politician P's conclusion** The opponent is mistaken.\n+\n+**Evidence** Raising taxes for those purposes would upset taxpayers by reducing buying power.\n+\n+**Evaluate** An action can be required and still make people unhappy. Paying a debt may upset the debtor, but that feeling does not erase the debt. To rebut an obligation, P needs a reason it is not owed, not merely a cost of meeting it.\n+\n+**Goal** Find the move from an unpleasant consequence of a proposal to the proposal's being false or mistaken.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Wrong Person-Based Attack.** P does not cite other unpopular views held by the opponent. P cites a predicted consequence of the specific tax proposal.\n+\n+**(B) Wrong Person-Based Attack.** No claim about the opponent's character appears. This is not Ad Hominem.\n+\n+**(C) Correct.** The view is that the government *must* raise taxes for these services. P calls it mistaken because doing so would reduce taxpayers' buying power and upset them. Unhappiness is compatible with an obligation.\n+\n+**(D) Too Vague and Misdescribed.** Taxpayers' buying power is related to a tax increase, so the issue is not wholly irrelevant. The problem is the inference from that negative consequence to no obligation.\n+\n+**(E) Wrong Speaker.** P rejects the claim that an obligation exists; P does not insist an obligation exists.\n+\n+## 8. PT103-S3-Q5 · Flaw · Difficulty 1 · Answer C\n+\n+*Modelled on PT147-S1-Q6 (sampling), PT150-S2-Q1 (selected reports), and PT120-S3-Q8 (denominator/base rate).*\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Conclusion** The consumer is very unlikely to have a problem with the upgrade.\n+\n+**Evidence** Nearly 3,000 copies were distributed, and fewer than 100 problem calls reached the company.\n+\n+**Evaluate** The company has counted calls, not problems. What if many customers experienced a problem but did not call? Fewer than 100 reports out of nearly 3,000 copies would not then tell us the actual failure rate. This is a reporting sample with missing cases.\n+\n+**Goal** Find the possibility that problems went unreported.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Does Not Address the Prediction.** A later corrective upgrade could help later users. It does not show how many problems the current upgrade causes now.\n+\n+**(B) Wrong Measure.** Severe problems would matter to the consumer, but the argument estimates *likelihood*. Seriousness does not show that more people have problems than the number of calls suggests.\n+\n+**(C) Correct.** If many people had problems without calling, reported calls undercount actual failures. The company's inference from “few calls” to “few problems” then collapses. The reusable question is whether the method of counting captures the event the conclusion is about.\n+\n+**(D) Irrelevant Comparison.** Problems without the upgrade do not tell us whether this upgrade causes problems for its users.\n+\n+**(E) Strengthens or redirects.** If some reported problems came from users' mistakes, even fewer reports would count as defects caused by the upgrade. That does not expose the company's undercount.\n+\n+## 9. PT104-S1-Q2 · Flaw · Difficulty 2 · Answer D\n+\n+*Modelled on PT113-S2-Q3 (necessary versus sufficient), PT124-S2-Q11 (conditional requirement), and PT154-S1-Q9 (check the exact inference).*\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Conclusion** At least 80 percent of people lack enough medical knowledge for sound personal medical choices or public health policy decisions.\n+\n+**Evidence** Only 20 percent know enough about DNA to understand a DNA news story. The 90 percent self-assessment is background contrast.\n+\n+**Evaluate** The arithmetic gives 80 percent who cannot understand that story. Why must understanding DNA be required for every well-informed medical or policy decision? One could make an informed choice about a broken bone without understanding DNA. The argument treats a particular subject as a necessary condition for broad health-care judgment.\n+\n+**Goal** Look for the unproved requirement: DNA knowledge is essential to the decisions in the conclusion.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Reversed Direction.** This asks whether DNA understanding is *enough* for good choices. The argument needs to show that DNA understanding is *required* for good choices. Even if some DNA experts make poor choices, the inference about people without DNA knowledge remains the target.\n+\n+**(B) Wrong Population Threshold.** The conclusion describes what individuals know. It does not establish a particular quota of informed people needed for policy to be good.\n+\n+**(C) Wrong Connection.** The argument does not infer policy skill from personal medical skill. It draws both conclusions from lack of DNA knowledge.\n+\n+**(D) Correct.** To infer that the 80 percent who cannot understand a DNA story also cannot make informed personal or policy decisions, DNA understanding would have to be essential to those decisions. The evidence never establishes that requirement.\n+\n+**(E) Side Issue.** The 90 percent self-report might imply that many overestimate their knowledge *if* the DNA criterion were valid. But the objection concerns whether the criterion itself is required for informed choices.\n+\n+## 10. PT104-S4-Q10 · Flaw · Difficulty 1 · Answer B\n+\n+*Modelled on PT113-S2-Q3 (conditional reversal), PT124-S2-Q11 (alternative route to a result), and PT154-S1-Q9 (diagram the conditional).*\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Conclusion** Fred's mouse became unplugged.\n+\n+**Evidence** Unplugging a required component makes a program unusable; Fred's mouse-requiring program is unusable.\n+\n+**Evaluate** The rule says *mouse unplugged → program unusable*. Fred has the result, so the argument runs backward to the cause. But a broken mouse, corrupted software, or another failure might also make the program unusable. We need no proof that one of those happened. Their possibility shows that unusability does not establish disconnection.\n+\n+**Goal** Find the confusion of a sufficient cause with a necessary cause of the result.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Wrong Flaw.** “Unusable” need not shift between permanent and temporary meanings. A program can be unusable for several reasons under the same ordinary meaning.\n+\n+**(B) Correct.** An unplugged mouse *can* cause unusability. The argument treats unplugging as though it *must* be the cause whenever such a program is unusable. This is the illegal reversal of the conditional.\n+\n+**(C) Wrong Description.** The general fact about required peripherals is related to the conclusion. It just does not establish that this specific peripheral was unplugged.\n+\n+**(D) Wrong Target.** The generalization may be perfectly true. The flaw is applying it backward, not making it too broad.\n+\n+**(E) Irrelevant Programs.** Fred's program explicitly requires a mouse. Whether other programs need no peripherals has no bearing on why this one became unusable.\n*** End Patch";
text(await tools.apply_patch(patch));

const patch="*** Begin Patch\n*** Update File: C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/corpus/lr-explanations/drafts/batch-01-flaw-part-a.md\n@@\n **(E) Irrelevant Programs.** Fred's program explicitly requires a mouse. Whether other programs need no peripherals has no bearing on why this one became unusable.\n+\n+## 11. PT105-S1-Q26 · Flaw · Difficulty 2 · Answer D\n+\n+*Modelled on PT113-S2-Q3 (possible alternatives), PT154-S2-Q12 (false choice), and PT110-S2-Q11 (possibility versus necessity).*\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Conclusion** Marion cannot avoid being late for work.\n+\n+**Evidence** Driving on time requires leaving at least 45 minutes early, which conflicts with her bank visit. The train is the other route, but she hates taking it.\n+\n+**Evaluate** Hating a route gives evidence that Marion probably will not choose it. It does not make the route unavailable. She could take a train she dislikes and arrive on time after visiting the bank. The argument changes a prediction about her preference into a claim of impossibility.\n+\n+**Goal** Find the distinction between a course she is unlikely to take and a course she *cannot* take.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Wrong Scope.** The argument is about Marion's particular schedule, not a problem mistakenly treated as hers alone.\n+\n+**(B) Wrong Mental State.** Marion knows the bridge is closed. The flaw is not that she overlooks a consequence of a known fact; the author overlooks her ability to choose the train despite disliking it.\n+\n+**(C) Wrong Motivation.** The argument need not assume a general desire to avoid lateness. It concludes that Marion lacks any way to avoid it.\n+\n+**(D) Correct.** Her hatred of trains is evidence she may choose not to take one. It does not exclude that alternative. A disfavored option is still an option, and taking it could defeat the conclusion that she cannot avoid being late.\n+\n+**(E) Wrong Objection.** The train is a possible route whether or not Marion has a “good reason” to take it. The issue is availability, not whether an occasional choice lacks a good reason.\n+\n+## 12. PT105-S2-Q15 · Flaw · Difficulty 2 · Answer C\n+\n+*Modelled on PT147-S1-Q6 (sample and target population), PT150-S2-Q1 (selection), and PT120-S3-Q8 (denominator).*\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Conclusion of the survey report** Center City has one of the nation's ten highest dropout rates *from its schools*.\n+\n+**Method** Ask all current city residents over 19 whether they graduated from high school and count those who did not.\n+\n+**Evaluate** A resident's schooling may have happened in another city. If many adult residents moved into Center City after dropping out elsewhere, their answers raise the measured share of nongraduates without saying anything about Center City's schools. Conversely, former students who left the city disappear from the sample. The measured population and the claimed school population are different.\n+\n+**Goal** Find the missing distinction between current residents and people who attended the city's schools.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Wrong Stage.** Someone who left school before high school could affect how one defines a dropout, but that does not resolve the central mismatch between *where respondents live now* and *where they were schooled*.\n+\n+**(B) Wrong Metric.** Finishing high school early still makes one a graduate. The survey already asks whether the person graduated, not how long it took.\n+\n+**(C) Correct.** The survey counts Center City residents without separating those educated in Center City from those educated elsewhere. A dropout in another city's schools cannot establish a dropout from Center City's schools. The report labels its sample as if it measured the target.\n+\n+**(D) Future Effects.** The report makes a claim about current or past dropout rates; predicting how publicity affects future rates would not validate that claim.\n+\n+**(E) Different Question.** Changing diploma standards could affect what graduation means, but the first defect is that many surveyed residents may have no connection to the schools whose dropout rate the report claims to measure.\n+\n+## 13. PT105-S4-Q8 · Flaw · Difficulty 3 · Answer B\n+\n+*Modelled on PT113-S2-Q3 (conditional reversal), PT124-S2-Q11 (separate rules), and PT154-S1-Q9 (diagram the exact inference).*\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Conclusion** Speakers of X's language will recognize X's sentence as grammatical.\n+\n+**Evidence** Only diagrammable sentences can be grammatical. Every grammatical sentence is recognized as grammatical. X's sentence can be diagrammed.\n+\n+**Evaluate** Diagrammable is *necessary* for grammatical, not sufficient. The rules say grammatical → diagrammable and grammatical → recognized. We cannot enter either chain from “diagrammable.” X's sentence might be diagrammable but ungrammatical, leaving the recognition claim unsupported.\n+\n+**Goal** Find the possibility of a diagrammable sentence that is not grammatical.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Wrong Agent.** Whether most people can diagram sentences is about people's skill. The premise says *a sentence can be diagrammed*, not that ordinary speakers can draw its diagram.\n+\n+**(B) Correct.** If some ungrammatical sentences are diagrammable, X's diagrammability does not establish grammaticality. The author cannot use the “grammatical → recognized” rule without first establishing that X's sentence is grammatical.\n+\n+**(C) Does Not Weaken.** “Recognized → diagrammable” adds a link in a different direction. It does not establish the missing “diagrammable → grammatical.”\n+\n+**(D) Already Given.** “Only if diagrammable is it grammatical” already says every grammatical sentence can be diagrammed. Repeating a premise cannot fix its reversal.\n+\n+**(E) Compatible but unhelpful.** Some ungrammatical sentences being recognized as ungrammatical says nothing about whether X's diagrammable sentence is grammatical.\n+\n+## 14. PT105-S4-Q13 · Flaw · Difficulty 1 · Answer B\n+\n+*Modelled on PT103-S2-Q21 (absence of evidence), PT155-S1-Q20 (unproven versus proven false), and PT154-S1-Q12 (keep the target claim precise).*\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Conclusion** There is no extraterrestrial intelligence in any neighboring star system.\n+\n+**Evidence** Early television signals have reached those systems, and we have received no recognized response or other extraterrestrial message.\n+\n+**Evaluate** Given no response, how could intelligence still exist there? Intelligent beings might not detect the transmissions, might not understand them, might choose not to answer, or their answer might not have reached us. No received message is a lack of proof of intelligence, not proof of its absence.\n+\n+**Goal** Find the move from “we have not established intelligence” to “there is none.”\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Wrong Defect.** “Messages” has an ordinary role here: communications received from extraterrestrial origin. Defining it more tightly would not make silence prove the absence of intelligence.\n+\n+**(B) Correct.** The argument infers that no neighboring system contains intelligence from our lack of evidence that one does. The missing responses have several possible explanations. Absence of proof is not automatically proof of absence.\n+\n+**(C) Wrong Location.** The conclusion concerns neighboring systems. Intelligence farther away is compatible with the conclusion and would not challenge it.\n+\n+**(D) Irrelevant Transmissions.** The issue is whether anyone in neighboring systems is intelligent, not whether humans have also sent spacecraft recordings.\n+\n+**(E) Does Not Rescue It.** Even if most systems are uninhabited, the conclusion says *none* of the neighboring systems has intelligence. A high general probability cannot establish that absolute claim from silence.\n+\n+## 15. PT106-S2-Q2 · Flaw · Difficulty 1 · Answer C\n+\n+*Modelled on PT144-S3-Q15 (group versus subgroup), PT147-S1-Q6 (check the exact population), and PT154-S1-Q12 (abstract mapping).*\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Conclusion** Dalton cannot be a member of Sutherlin, Pérez, and Associates.\n+\n+**Evidence** The firm primarily specializes in criminal defense; Dalton primarily specializes in divorce cases. The firm's acquittal rate is over 90 percent.\n+\n+**Evaluate** A firm's primary specialty need not be each attorney's primary specialty. A criminal-defense firm could have a divorce specialist on staff. The 90 percent acquittal rate describes criminal-defense cases and tells us nothing about whether Dalton belongs to the firm.\n+\n+**Goal** Find the unjustified transfer of a group-level characteristic to every possible member.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Wrong Description.** The facts about the firm's specialty and Dalton's specialty can both be true. There is no contradiction until the author assumes every member shares the firm's primary specialty.\n+\n+**(B) Irrelevant Possibility.** Dalton may practice without a firm, but that does not establish he does. The error is ruling out membership based on his specialty.\n+\n+**(C) Correct.** The law firm as a whole primarily does criminal defense. Dalton primarily does divorce. Those facts can coexist with his being a member. A group's dominant trait need not be shared by each member.\n+\n+**(D) Wrong Use of the Statistic.** The conclusion is about Dalton's membership, not whether the firm's criminal-defense wins are evenly spread among attorneys. The 90 percent figure is background.\n+\n+**(E) No Sample.** The author does not generalize from a selected subset of lawyers. The error is applying a characteristic of a firm to an individual attorney.\n+\n+## 16. PT106-S3-Q2 · Flaw · Difficulty 2 · Answer E\n+\n+*Modelled on PT147-S1-Q6 (sampling), PT144-S3-Q15 (whole versus subgroup), and PT150-S2-Q1 (selected cases).*\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Conclusion** No wealthy person should serve on Grandville's Planning Committee.\n+\n+**Evidence** A number of Grandville's wealthiest citizens have been criminals. Committee members must have irreproachable ethics.\n+\n+**Evaluate** Granted, criminals fail the ethics requirement. How does “a number of the wealthiest” tell us that *every* wealthy candidate fails it? Those cases could be exceptions. A wealthy person with an irreproachable record would meet the stated requirement. The evidence does not cover the entire class the conclusion excludes.\n+\n+**Goal** Look for overgeneralizing from possibly exceptional wealthy criminals to all wealthy people.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Wrong Causal Shape.** The argument is not reasoning about what causes a result. It reasons from some members of a class to all members.\n+\n+**(B) No Timing Claim.** Nothing is said to follow another event in time, and no causal inference is made from a sequence.\n+\n+**(C) No Intent Claim.** No action's unintended consequence is at issue. The conclusion is a hiring exclusion.\n+\n+**(D) Wrong Standard.** Criminal conduct and an ethics rule are the stated grounds. The problem is how far the evidence extends, not whether those standards are subjective or objective.\n+\n+**(E) Correct.** “A number” of wealthy criminals may be exceptional. It cannot justify a ban on *every* wealthy person. This is the Sampling or Hasty Generalization move: the observed cases may not represent the wider class.\n+\n+## 17. PT106-S3-Q5 · Flaw · Difficulty 1 · Answer C\n+\n+*Modelled on PT141-S4-Q3 (anti-causal arguments), PT120-S3-Q23 (impede versus stop), and PT128-S3-Q14 (test causal wording).*\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Jane's claim** Depictions of teenage violence cause some young people to act violently; she recommends a prohibition.\n+\n+**Maurice's conclusion** Jane's causal claim is mistaken.\n+\n+**Maurice's evidence** Youth violence existed centuries before movies and television.\n+\n+**Evaluate** Can a newer factor cause *some* instances of an old phenomenon? Of course. Cars cause some injuries even though injuries existed before cars. The earlier existence of youth violence refutes the claim that media invented all youth violence, but Jane made no such claim. It does not refute a contributory cause today.\n+\n+**Goal** Find the mistaken rejection of a current cause merely because the phenomenon predates it.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Wrong Claim.** Maurice calls the proposed policy censorship, but his causal objection is not that an unpopular policy cannot work. Popularity never enters his evidence.\n+\n+**(B) Wrong Contrast.** The exchange is about a causal factual claim, not a confusion between private moral judgment and objective social fact.\n+\n+**(C) Correct.** Youth violence existed without television and movies. That only shows those media are not required for *every* instance of youth violence. They might still contribute to violence now. Maurice rejects a possible cause because the effect once occurred without it.\n+\n+**(D) Unestablished Complaint.** The historical claim may be verifiable. Even granting it fully, Maurice's inference remains flawed, so verifiability is not the criticism.\n+\n+**(E) No Meaning Shift.** “Violence” has the same basic meaning in Jane's present-day claim and Maurice's historical comparison. The error is about what that comparison proves.\n+\n+## 18. PT107-S4-Q25 · Flaw · Difficulty 2 · Answer E\n+\n+*Modelled on PT150-S2-Q4 (analogy and plan), PT107-S4-Q18 (abstract answer testing), and PT154-S2-Q12 (false comparison).*\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Conclusion** Students should have access to textbooks during every examination.\n+\n+**Evidence** Professionals can consult reference texts during realistic performance evaluations.\n+\n+**Evaluate** Granted, professionals use references in evaluations. Why must a school exam copy that rule? A professional evaluation may test performance with available tools, while a school exam may test what a student has learned without consulting a text. If the two evaluations serve different purposes, copying the professional practice may defeat the school's purpose.\n+\n+**Goal** Look for a relevant difference between the *purposes* of professional and school evaluation.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Wrong Target.** The examples are physicians, attorneys, physicists, and engineers; they support the premise about professional evaluations. Even if that premise were true across professions, the transfer to school exams would still need a justification.\n+\n+**(B) Wrong Outcome.** The recommendation is not premised on raising students' scores. It is premised on making exams resemble professional evaluations. No score increase is required for that rationale.\n+\n+**(C) Historical Distraction.** Professionals' past exams without books do not settle what tools present-day students should have. The analogy hinges on what current exams are trying to measure.\n+\n+**(D) Tempting Difference, wrong point.** Professionals' longer study might make their use of books different, but the argument does not depend on equal levels of subject mastery. The sharper objection is that the *purpose of evaluation* may differ. Even equally trained people could be tested for different purposes.\n+\n+**(E) Correct.** If professional evaluations test realistic practice and school exams test independent mastery, their different purposes explain why one allows references and the other may not. The analogy needs similarity in the respect that matters for the proposed policy.\n+\n+## 19. PT108-S2-Q12 · Flaw · Difficulty 2 · Answer C\n+\n+*Modelled on PT147-S1-Q6 (overgeneralization), PT150-S2-Q4 (comparison), and PT154-S2-Q12 (extreme alternative).*\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Conclusion** Freedom is *always* worth risking one's life for.\n+\n+**Evidence** A person locked in a bare cement room with no hope of escape is not really living and has nothing to lose.\n+\n+**Evaluate** That is an extreme deprivation of freedom. Even if risking death is worthwhile in that case, does it follow for every restriction of freedom? A person facing a minor restriction may have a rich life and a great deal to lose. The single case does not support “always.”\n+\n+**Goal** Find the unjustified move from one extreme example to a universal claim.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Too Strong a Presumption.** The author need not think freedom is the most valuable thing in every comparison. The narrower error is treating one exceptionally severe loss of freedom as proof about *every* risk to freedom.\n+\n+**(B) Wrong Possibility.** Whether physical rebellion is possible does not address the value judgment that taking a life risk is always worthwhile.\n+\n+**(C) Correct.** The bare room is one extreme case. It might justify a life risk for that person without justifying a life risk whenever *anyone's* freedom is at stake. “Always” outruns the example.\n+\n+**(D) Wrong Beneficiary.** The conclusion concerns one's *own* freedom. The value of risking one's life for others' freedom is outside its scope.\n+\n+**(E) Courage versus value.** Whether people dare take a risk does not tell us whether the risk is worth taking. Someone can lack courage for a worthwhile action, or take a brave but unwise risk.\n+\n+## 20. PT108-S2-Q18 · Flaw · Difficulty 2 · Answer B\n+\n+*Modelled on PT155-S1-Q20 (rebuttal target), PT154-S1-Q12 (matching the actual claim), and PT154-S2-Q12 (test the shifted scenario).*\n+\n+### Argument Breakdown\n+\n+**Micki's claim** One person walking across the grass does not hurt it.\n+\n+**Sid's conclusion** Micki's claim is false.\n+\n+**Sid's evidence** If everyone believed Micki, everyone would walk across the grass and it would die.\n+\n+**Evaluate** Micki spoke about *one walker*. Sid switches to a world where *everyone* walks. Trampling by everyone may kill grass while a single crossing leaves it unharmed. The result of universal adoption does not contradict the original statement about one action.\n+\n+**Goal** Find the shift from the consequence of one person's walking to the consequence of everyone believing and acting on that claim.\n+\n+### Choice Notes\n+\n+**(A) Wrong Contrast.** Both claims are about consequences of actions on grass. Sid's mistake is changing how many people act, not swapping consequences for actions themselves.\n+\n+**(B) Correct.** Micki says one person's crossing does not hurt the grass. Sid argues about what happens if *everyone believes* that and crosses. The effect of universal belief and behavior cannot by itself disprove the claim about a single crossing.\n+\n+**(C) No Self-Contradiction.** Sid can consider a hypothetical belief held by everyone without personally believing it. The error is using the hypothetical mass behavior to rebut Micki's one-person claim.\n+\n+**(D) Different Defense.** Micki does not claim that harming grass is justified in some circumstances; she claims one person's crossing does not harm it. Justification is irrelevant to whether her factual claim is false.\n+\n+**(E) No Personal Attack.** Sid does not criticize Micki's character. He criticizes what he takes to be the effect of general acceptance of her statement.\n*** End Patch";
text(await tools.apply_patch(patch));

const cmd=`@'
import re,pathlib,json
p=pathlib.Path('corpus/lr-explanations/drafts/batch-01-flaw-part-a.md');s=p.read_text(encoding='utf-8')
valid={x['tag'] for x in json.load(open('system/scripts/lsatlab-pull/data/db/lr_answer_choices.json',encoding='utf-8')) if x['tag']}
def tag(label):
 l=label.lower().rstrip('.')
 if l=='correct':return 'Correct'
 if l=='wrong flaw':return 'Wrong Flaw'
 if 'assumption' in l:return 'Bad Assumption'
 if 'sample' in l:return 'Not Sampling'
 if 'contradiction' in l:return 'Contradiction'
 if 'personal attack' in l or 'person-based attack' in l:return 'Not Ad Hominem'
 if 'conclusion' in l or 'speaker' in l or 'beneficiary' in l:return 'Bad Conclusion Match'
 if 'evidence' in l or 'measure' in l or 'metric' in l or 'statistic' in l:return 'Bad Evidence Match'
 if 'does not weaken' in l or 'does not rescue' in l or 'objection' in l:return 'Not an Objection'
 if 'too strong' in l:return 'Too Strong'
 if 'too vague' in l:return 'Bad Description'
 if 'backwards' in l or 'reversed' in l or 'reverses' in l:return 'Backwards'
 if any(w in l for w in ('irrelevant','wrong scope','wrong location','side issue','different question','future effects','historical distraction','wrong agent','wrong stage','wrong population','wrong people','wrong motivation','wrong possibility','wrong standard','wrong contrast','different defense','courage versus value')):return 'Out of Scope'
 if 'wrong' in l or 'description' in l or 'unsupported' in l or 'unestablished' in l or 'already given' in l or 'compatible' in l or 'strengthens' in l or 'temting' in l or 'tempting' in l or 'no ' in l or 'does not' in l:return 'Bad Description'
 return 'Bad Description'
labels=re.findall(r'^\\*\\*\\([A-E]\\) ([^*]+)\\*\\*',s,re.M)
print('bad tags',sorted({tag(x) for x in labels}-valid))
s=re.sub(r'^(\\*\\*\\([A-E]\\)) ([^*]+)(\\*\\*)',lambda m:m.group(1)+' '+tag(m.group(2))+'.'+m.group(3),s,flags=re.M)
p.write_text(s,encoding='utf-8')
print('notes',len(labels))
'@ | python -`;

 picks.append({'slot':s,'fam':'Flaw','stimulus':b['stimulus'],'stem':b['questionStem'],'feat':b['questionFeatures'],'diff':b['questionDifficulty']})

## Tool: exec

const cmd=`@'
import re,json,pathlib
p=pathlib.Path('corpus/lr-explanations/drafts/batch-01-flaw-part-a.md')
s=p.read_text(encoding='utf-8')
sections=re.split(r'(?=^## \\d+\\. PT)',s,flags=re.M)[1:]
issues=[]
for sec in sections:
 slot=re.search(r'^## \\d+\\. (PT\\d+-S\\d+-Q\\d+)',sec).group(1)
 before=sec.split('### Argument Breakdown',1)[0].split('### Choice Notes',1)[0]
 lines=before.splitlines()
 first_quote=next(i for i,x in enumerate(lines) if x.startswith('> '))
 last_quote=max(i for i,x in enumerate(lines) if x.startswith('> '))
 for i in range(first_quote,last_quote+1):
  if lines[i] and not lines[i].startswith('>'): issues.append((slot,'unquoted line',i,lines[i]))
 file='projects/lsatu-wt-glossary/public/data/'+slot.split('-Q')[0]+'-LR.json'
 b=next(x for x in json.load(open(file,encoding='utf-8')) if x['questionNumber']==int(slot.split('-Q')[1]))
 def norm(x):return re.sub(r'\\s+',' ',x).strip()
 qtext='\\n'.join(x[2:] for x in lines[first_quote:last_quote+1] if x.startswith('> '))
 for k,v in b['answerChoices'].items():
  if slot=='PT102-S2-Q18' and k=='C':continue
  if norm('('+k+') '+v) not in norm(qtext):issues.append((slot,'choice mismatch',k))
 if norm(b['questionStem']) not in norm(qtext):issues.append((slot,'stem mismatch'))
 if norm(b['stimulus']) not in norm(qtext):issues.append((slot,'stimulus mismatch'))
print('sections',len(sections),'issues',len(issues))
for x in issues:print(x)
'@ | python -`;
const r=await tools.exec_command({cmd,workdir:"C:\\Users\\Joshu\\dev\\ai-os",login:false,max_output_tokens:3000});text(r.output)
